The typical structure of dense claw-free graphs
Will Perkins, Sam van der Poel

Abstract
. We analyze the asymptotic number and typical structure of claw-free graphs at constant edge densities. The first of our main results is a formula for the asymptotics of the logarithm of the number of claw-free graphs of edge density γ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \gamma\in(0,1) . We show that the problem exhibits a second-order phase transition at edge density γ ∗ = 5 − 5 4 \gamma^{\ast}=\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4} . The asymptotic formula arises by solving a variational problem over graphons. For γ ⩾ γ ∗ \gamma\geqslant\gamma^{\ast} there is a unique optimal graphon, while for γ < γ ∗ \gamma<\gamma^{\ast} there is an infinite set of optimal graphons. By analyzing more detailed structure, we prove that for γ < γ ∗ \gamma<\gamma^{\ast} , there is in fact a unique graphon W W such that almost all claw-free graphs at edge density γ \gamma are close in cut metric to W W . We also analyze the probability of claw-freeness in the Erdős–Rényi random graph G ⁡ ( n , p ) G(n,p) for constant p p , obtaining a formula for the large-deviation rate function for claw-freeness. In this case, the problem exhibits a first-order phase transition at p ∗ = 3 − 5 2 p^{\ast}=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2} , separating distinct structural regimes. At the critical point p ∗ p^{\ast} , the corresponding graphon variational problem has infinitely many solutions, and we again pinpoint a unique optimal graphon that describes the typical structure of G ⁡ ( n , p ∗ ) G(n,p^{\ast}) conditioned on being claw-free.



1.
Introduction

A graph is
claw-free
if none of its induced subgraphs is isomorphic to
K_{1,3}
. In this paper we study the
evolution of structure
of dense claw-free graphs: how does the structure of a typical claw-free graph change as its edge density varies? First, we derive formulas for the asymptotics of the logarithm of the number of claw-free graphs at constant edge densities and the asymptotics of the logarithm of the probability that the Erdős–Rényi random graph
G(n,p)
is claw-free for constant
p
. The formulas arise from variational problems over the space of graphons, and we show that the solutions to these problems exhibit phase transitions. The optimal graphons provide a rough structural description of claw-free graphs, and after a more detailed analysis, we make finer structural statements and derive more precise asymptotic formulas.

A classical example of asymptotic enumeration and typical structure of constrained graphs is the case of triangle-free graphs.
[
47
]
proved in
[
47
]
that the complete balanced bipartite graph is the extremal triangle-free graph, i.e. the one with the most edges
[
47
]
. In fact, the logarithm of the number of subgraphs of the extremal graph is asymptotic to the logarithm of the number of triangle-free graphs.
[
28
]
proved the much stronger result that almost all
1
1

1

All except a fraction that tends to zero as
n\to\infty
.
triangle-free graphs are bipartite
[
28
]
; and so by asymptotically enumerating bipartite graphs, one obtains an asymptotic formula for the number of triangle-free graphs. Many works have also studied how the structure of a typical triangle-free graph depends on the edge density
[
61
,
45
,
54
,
9
]
. Related questions ask for the probability
G(n,p)
is triangle-free and the typical structure of
G(n,p)
conditioned on being triangle-free, the answers to which are known for constant
p
and some regimes of
p=o(1)

[
34
,
61
,
45
]
.

To derive typical structure results, it has proven useful to study graphons, the limit objects of large dense graphs
[
42
,
15
]
.
[
18
]
proved an important result in this regard: a large deviation principle for the Erdős–Rényi random graph in which the rate function is given by a variational problem over graphons
[
18
]
. The optima and optimizers of this variational problem are informative about the number and structure of graphs in a broad range of properties defined by subgraph density constraints. An important example, and one that predates the study of graphons, is the case of
H
-free graphs where
H
is a constant-sized subgraph and
\chi(H)=r+1\geqslant 3
. A typical
H
-free graph of edge density
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{r-1}{r}\big)
is within
o(n^{2})
edit distance of a nearly balanced
r
-partite graph with edge density approximately
\frac{r}{r-1}\gamma
between color classes (see
[
16
]
for a proof based on the earlier work of
[
26
]

[
26
]
), and note that the range
\gamma>\frac{r-1}{r}
is excluded by the Erdős–Stone theorem. In the language of graphons, the variational problem over
H
-free graphons with edge density
\gamma
has a unique
2
2

2

Uniqueness here is meant up to equivalence of graphons, which is discussed in more detail in
Section

1.1
.
optimizer with an
r\times r
block structure, density
\frac{r}{r-1}\gamma
in off-diagonal blocks, and density 0 in diagonal blocks. Notably, the solution to this variational problem maintains this structure and the optimum varies smoothly with
\gamma
; that is, there is no phase transition.

More recently, the lower- and upper-tail large deviation problems have received much attention: for
\delta>0
, what is the probability that the number of copies of a fixed graph
H
in
G(n,p)
is at most
1-\delta
or at least
1+\delta
times the expected number? For constant
p
, the logarithmic asymptotics of the lower- and upper-tail probabilities, i.e. the large deviation rate functions, are given by the variational problem of
[
18
]
. For the upper-tail variational problem,
[
43
]
identified the ‘replica symmetric’ regime for cliques and more generally
d
-regular
H
; that is, the set of parameters for which the optimizing graphon is constant
[
43
]
(see also work on the variational problem for
p=o(1)

[
44
]
). For the lower-tail variational problem for triangles,
[
66
]
showed that for small
\delta
the optimal graphon is constant while for larger
\delta
it is not
[
66
]
. In both cases, these solutions show that a phase transition (in the sense of a non-analyticity of the rate function) occurs as
\delta
(or
p
) varies. A related line of research studies the variational problem with fixed subgraph densities; for example,
[
64
]
proved that edge- and triangle-constrained graphs exhibit a phase transition and fully characterized the optimizers for certain positive edge and triangle density pairs
[
63
,
64
]
. Some works have shown that in the sparse case
p=o(1)
, the lower- and upper-tail large deviation problems still reduce to certain variational problems
[
17
,
25
,
22
,
6
,
30
,
40
]
; in this case the upper-tail problem is completely solved for cliques
[
44
]
, while the lower-tail problem is still widely open
[
66
]
.

In this paper, we fully characterize the entropy density of claw-free graphs for constant edge densities and the large deviation rate function for claw-freeness in
G(n,p)
for constant
p
. In contrast to the problem of
H
-freeness described above, phase transitions occur in both problems; in the case of entropy density the phase transition is second-order, while in the case of the rate function, the phase transition is first-order. We obtain these results by solving variational problems over claw-free graphons. In both cases, for certain parameter regimes there are infinitely many distinct optimizers, yet in all cases can still identify the unique typical structure (in the sense of cut metric) of claw-free graphs using a finer analysis of the counts and probabilities. These more fine-grained results advance the research direction set in motion by
[
57
]
of proving strong typical structure results for induced-
H
-free graphs
[
57
]
.

1.1.
Main Results

Let
{\mathcal{C}}(n)
be the set of claw-free graphs on
n
vertices, and let
{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
be the set of claw-free graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges. The binary entropy
H:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the function
H(x)=-x\log_{2}(x)-(1-x)\log_{2}(1-x)
with the convention
0\log_{2}0=0
. Define
r^{\ast}:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

(1)

r^{\ast}(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{10}H\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big($}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big($}}}}}\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\displaystyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\textstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\big)$}}}}}{\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\resizebox{327680}{786432}{\hbox{\raisebox{3.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\big)$}}}}}\gamma&\gamma\in\big[0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)\\[5.0pt]
\frac{1}{2}H(2\gamma-1)&\gamma\in\big[\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big]\end{cases}\,.

If
{\mathcal{P}}(n)
is a set of graphs on
n
vertices, defined for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then the
entropy density
of
{\mathcal{P}}(n)
is defined to be the number
\lim_{n\to\infty}\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\log_{2}|{\mathcal{P}}(n)|
, provided the limit exists
3
3

3

The limit exists in particular for hereditary properties
[
1
,
12
]
and a wide range of properties defined by subgraph density constraints
[
18
]
.
. Our first main result states that
r^{\ast}(\gamma)
is the entropy density of
{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
be a fixed constant and let
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. If
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}|{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)|=r^{\ast}(\gamma)\,.

The graph of
r^{\ast}(\gamma)
is depicted in the first plot of
Figure

1
. Note
r^{\ast}(3/4)=1/2
is the entropy density of
{\mathcal{C}}(n)
(known by prior work of
[
58
]

[
58
]
); a theorem of
[
8
]

[
8
]
implies almost all claw-free graphs are co-bipartite, so the value
r^{\ast}(3/4)=1/2
reflects the fact that almost all co-bipartite graphs have edge density approximately
3/4
.

The entropy density
r^{\ast}(\gamma)
has a continuous first derivative and discontinuous second derivative, so there is a second-order phase transition at
\gamma^{\ast}=\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}
(see
[
32
]
for background on the classification of phase transitions).

Next, define
r_{\ast}(p):[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

(2)

r_{\ast}(p)=\begin{cases}-\log_{2}(1-p)&p\in\big[0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\big)\\[5.0pt]
-\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}p&p\in\big[\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\big]\end{cases}\,.

Our second main result states that
r_{\ast}(p)
is the large deviation rate function for the event that
G(n,p)
is claw-free.

Theorem 1.2
.

If
p\in(0,1)
is a fixed constant,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
G\sim G(n,p)
then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n)\}=-r_{\ast}(p)\,.

The graph of
r_{\ast}(p)
is depicted in the second plot of
Figure

1
. The case
p=1/2
corresponds with counting claw-free graphs, so the rate function recovers the known entropy density of
{\mathcal{C}}(n)
as a special case. Since
r_{\ast}(p)
has a discontinuous first derivative, there is a first-order phase transition at
p^{\ast}=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
. As we will show in the structural results below, the first-order phase transition is also reflected in a discontinuity at
p^{\ast}
in the typical edge density of the conditioned Erdős–Rényi random graph. We also note that the rate function
r_{\ast}(p)
is non-monotone, which differs from what one observes for monotone graph properties.

We briefly provide some intuition for the forms of the functions
r^{\ast}(\gamma)
and
r_{\ast}(p)
. For
\gamma\in\big[\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big]
, the quantity
r^{\ast}(\gamma)
is entropy density of the set
{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)
of co-bipartite graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges. For
\gamma\in\big[0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
, the quantity
r^{\ast}(\gamma)
is the entropy density of the set of graphs
G
on
n
vertices and
m
edges that are the disjoint union of a co-bipartite graph with parts of sizes approximately
\big(\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{20}\gamma\big)^{1/2}n
and an empty graph on the remaining vertices. The density of edges between the two cliques in such a graph
G
is approximately
\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
. For
p\in\big[\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\big]
, the quantity
r_{\ast}(p)
is the large deviation rate function for the event that
G(n,p)
is co-bipartite, and for
p\in\big[0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\big)
,
r_{\ast}(p)
is the rate function for the event that
G(n,p)
has no edges.

The functions
r^{\ast}(\gamma)
and
r_{\ast}(p)
are the solutions to variational problems over graphons, which are measurable functions
W:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
satisfying
W(x,y)=W(y,x)
for all
x,\,y
. The set of all graphons is denoted
{\mathcal{W}}
. Graphons are representations of
graph limits
, which are the limits of large graphs with respect to the
cut metric
(these terms are introduced formally in
Section

2
; see
[
41
]
for more background). We say that two graphons are
equivalent
if they represent the same graph limit
4
4

4

[
14
]
proved
[
14
, Corollary 2.2]
that graphons
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
are equivalent if and only if there exist measure-preserving maps
\sigma_{i}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that
W_{1}(\sigma_{1}(x),\sigma_{1}(y))=W_{2}(\sigma_{2}(x),\sigma_{2}(y))
almost everywhere. See also
[
41
, Theorem 13.10]
.
.

For
\gamma\in(0,1)
, define the following variational problem over graphons
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\phi(\gamma)\hskip 1.42262pt:=&\sup&\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2}}H(W(x,y))\,dx\,dy\\[16.0pt]
&\text{s.t.}&\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2}}W(x,y)\,dx\,dy=\gamma\,,\\[12.0pt]
&&\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{4}}\Bigg(\prod_{i=2}^{4}W(x_{1},x_{i})\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{2\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4}(1-W(x_{i},x_{j}))\Bigg)\,dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,dx_{3}\,dx_{4}=0\,.\end{array}

The first constraint asserts that
W
has edge density
\gamma
. The second constraint asserts that
W
has zero claw-density; for the reader familiar with the
W
-random graph
G(n,W)
, defined formally later, this is equivalent to saying that
G(n,W)
is claw-free with probability 1. Our next result states the solution to the variational problem
\phi(\gamma)
and describes the number of graphons achieving the supremum.

Theorem 1.3
.

For all
\gamma\in(0,1)
we have
\phi(\gamma)=r^{\ast}(\gamma)
. For all
\gamma\in\big[\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big)
, there is a unique graphon (up to equivalence) achieving
\phi(\gamma)
, and for all
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
, there is an infinite number of distinct (nonequivalent) graphons achieving
\phi(\gamma)
.

By a slight extension of the Chatterjee–Varadhan framework to fixed-density induced-
H
-free graphons,
Proposition

2.11
proves
\phi(\gamma)
is the entropy density of
{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
. Thus from
Theorem

1.3
and
Proposition

2.11
we can deduce
Theorem

1.1
.

, and

, respectively.

In the range
\gamma\in\big[\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big)
, the unique graphon achieving
\phi(\gamma)
has the structure of a co-bipartite graph; see
Figure

2
for a depiction and
Equation

8
for a formal definition. In the range
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
, the optimal graphons have the structure of a graph that is the vertex-disjoint union of several co-bipartite graphs and a graph with
o(n^{2})
edges; see
Figure

3
for a depiction and
Equation

7
for a formal definition of these graphons.

For all
p\in(0,1)
define the relative entropy

(3)

I_{p}(x):=x\log_{2}\frac{p}{x}+(1-x)\log_{2}\frac{1-p}{1-x}=H(x)+x\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)+\log_{2}(1-p)\,,

and define the variational problem

\begin{array}[]{lll}\psi(p)\hskip 1.42262pt:=&\sup&\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{2}}I_{p}(W(x,y))\,dx\,dy\\[16.0pt]
&\text{s.t.}&\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{4}}\Bigg(\prod_{i=2}^{4}W(x_{1},x_{i})\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{2\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4}(1-W(x_{i},x_{j}))\Bigg)\,dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,dx_{3}\,dx_{4}=0\,.\end{array}

Our next result states that
r_{\ast}
is the solution to the variational problem
\psi
, and describes the number of graphons achieving the supremum.

Theorem 1.4
.

For all
p\in(0,1)
we have
\psi(p)=-r_{\ast}(p)
. For all
p\in\big(0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\big)
, there is a unique graphon (up to equivalence) achieving
\psi(p)
, for
p=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
, there is an infinite number of distinct (nonequivalent) graphons achieving
\psi(p)
, and for all
p\in\big(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\big)
, there is a unique graphon (up to equivalence) achieving
\psi(p)
.

In the range
p\in\big(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\big)
, the unique graphon achieving
\psi(p)
resembles the adjacency of a large co-bipartite graph with edge density
(1+p)/2
(see
Equation

19
for a formal definition). In the range
p\in\big(0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\big)
, the zero graphon is the unique optimal graphon. Finally, for
p=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
, the optimal graphons have various edge densities and resemble those in
Figure

3
.

[
18
]
showed that in addition to providing the rate function for
G(n,p)
, optimal graphons also give a rough structural description of the conditional Erdős–Rényi random graph. By a slight modification of their proof, we show that if
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
then almost every
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
is close in cut metric of an optimal graphon. Since the optimal graphons achieving
\phi(\gamma)
have a simple description—they have the structure of a disjoint union of co-bipartite graphs and a sparse graph—it is desirable to estimate the number of claw-free graphs that have precisely this simple structure. For example, does a statement analogous to the Erdős–Kleitman–Rothschild theorem hold for claw-free graphs with edge density greater than
\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}
? The next theorem answers this affirmatively and describes structure in the subcritical regime.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)
denote the set of co-bipartite graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges. For functions
f,g:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the notation
f(n)\sim g(n)
means
\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)/g(n)=1
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
be a fixed constant, let
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and assume
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. Then the following hold.

(1)

If
\gamma\in\big(\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big)
then almost all claw-free graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges are co-bipartite, that is,

|{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)|\sim|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|\sim\left(\frac{r+1}{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2\gamma-1)^{k^{2}+rk}\right)\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}\,,

where
r=n\bmod{2}
.

(2)

If
\gamma=\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}
and
m=\lfloor\gamma\binom{n}{2}\rfloor
then almost every
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
is the vertex-disjoint union of a co-bipartite graph and a graph with at most
\log n
vertices.

(3)

If
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
then almost every
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
is the vertex-disjoint union of two graphs
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
where
G_{1}
is a co-bipartite graph with parts of sizes

\Bigg(\sqrt{\frac{5+\smash[t]{\sqrt{5}}\rule{0.0pt}{9.25pt}}{20}\gamma}+o(1)\Bigg)n

and
G_{2}
has
\big(1-\big(\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{5}\gamma\big)^{1/2}+o(1)\big)n
vertices and
\Omega(n)\leqslant e(G_{2})\leqslant o(n^{2})
edges.

The asymptotic formula for
|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|
in
Theorem

1.5

1
is obtained by a standard calculation, included in
Appendix

C
.

In the subcritical regime
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
,
Theorem

1.5

3
implies there is a unique graphon
W^{\ast}
(up to equivalence) such that almost every
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
is within
o(1)
in cut metric of
W^{\ast}
. In fact, in
Section

6
we show that the number of claw-free graphs with edge density
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
is at least
n^{\Theta(n)}
times larger than the number of graphs that are the disjoint union of two or more co-bipartite graphs each having
\Theta(n)
vertices and a sparse graph, which are graphs close in cut metric to an optimal graphon besides
W^{\ast}
. For example, among the optimal graphons in
Figure

3
, there are at least
n^{\Theta(n)}
times as many claw-free graphs of edge density
1/4
that are close to the leftmost graphon as there are to any of the other three graphons.

Our last main result describes the typical structure of
G(n,p)
conditioned on being claw-free.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
p\in(0,1)
be a fixed constant and let
G
be the Erdős–Rényi random graph
G(n,p)
conditioned on being claw-free. Then the following hold.

(1)

If
p\in\big(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\big)
then
G
is co-bipartite with high probability.

(2)

If
p\in\big(0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\big]
then
G
has
o(n^{2})
edges with high probability.

The second part of the theorem shows that at the critical point
p^{\ast}=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
, although there are infinitely many optimal graphons as proven in
Theorem

1.4
, the conditional Erdős–Rényi converges in cut metric to the all-zero graphon in probability.

1.2.
Related Work

The study of asymptotic enumeration and typical structure has a long history in graph theory. Beyond the many works on triangle-free graphs mentioned above, there has been much research on analogous questions about
H
-free graphs for general graphs
H
with
r+1=\chi(H)\geqslant 3
. Using Szemerédi’s regularity lemma,
[
26
]
proved that the log-number of
H
-free graphs is asymptotic to the extremal number
\extremal(n,H)

[
26
]
.
[
59
]
proved that almost all
H
-free graphs are
r
-colorable if and only if
H
is edge-critical (i.e. the removal of an edge reduces the chromatic number)
[
59
]
, generalizing the Erdős–Kleitman–Rothschild theorem.
[
7
]
proved that if
{\mathcal{F}}
is a finite set of graphs and
r+1=\min_{H\in{\mathcal{F}}}\chi(H)\geqslant 3
, then almost all
{\mathcal{F}}
-free graphs are within
o(n^{2})
edit distance of an
r
-colorable graph
[
7
]
.

Several results for
H
-free graphs have induced-
H
-free analogues. For example,
[
2
]
proved a generalization of the Balogh–Bollobás–Simonovits theorem for hereditary properties
[
2
]
. Induced analogues of the Erdős–Stone theorem and Erdős–Frankl–Rödl theorem, proven by
[
60
]

[
58
,
60
]
, give an asymptotic formula for the log-number of induced-
H
-free graphs.

The study of typical structure of induced-
H
-free graphs was initiated by
[
57
]
, who examined induced-
C_{4}
-free graphs
[
57
]
. Recent works have also examined
sparse
induced-
C_{4}
-free graphs
[
36
,
52
]
.
[
8
]
proved an induced analogue to
[
59
]
’s result about edge-critical graphs: they defined a notion of criticality that characterizes the graphs
H
for which almost all induced-
H
-free graphs have a simple structure
[
8
]
.
[
50
]
proved first-order asymptotics for the number of cubic claw-free graphs
[
50
]
, which plays an important role in the proof of
Theorem

1.5

3
.
[
16
]
analyzed induced-
C_{5}
-free graphs, deriving the entropy density and showing it has two critical points
[
16
]
.

Prior works have also examined the probability that
G(n,p)
belongs to a general hereditary property
{\mathcal{H}}
. Using the extremal theory of 2-colored multigraphs,
[
49
]
proved that there is always an “elementary property”
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
contained in
{\mathcal{H}}
such that the rate function for the event
G(n,p)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
coincides with the rate function for the event
G(n,p)\in{\mathcal{H}}

[
49
]
.
[
49
]
also calculated the rate function for some specific hereditary properties including the set of induced-
C^{\ast}_{6}
-free graphs (where
C^{\ast}_{6}
is the cycle
C_{6}
with a diagonal chord). See also the related works
[
13
,
48
]
.

Graphons have been used extensively to study graph properties, random graphs, and typical structure. Many works have sought to solve the variational problem over graphons that are limits of constrained graph classes
[
63
,
62
,
5
,
43
,
37
,
38
,
66
,
68
,
53
]
. In this paper, we prove the existence of a phase in which there is more than one optimal graphon, but where the typical structure of the underlying property is still captured by a unique graphon; this phenomenon has previously been observed in string graphs
[
35
,
55
]
.

Besides the topics of this paper, there is broad interest in claw-free graphs in graph theory and theoretical computer science. Historically, interest in claw-free graphs originated with
[
10
]
’s characterization of line graphs in terms of forbidden induced subgraphs
[
10
]
.
Later,
[
19
]
proved a detailed characterization of claw-free graphs through decomposition theorems
[
19
,
21
]
, and showed that the independence polynomial of claw-free graphs is real-rooted (extending the Heilmann–Lieb theorem)
[
20
]
, which is relevant in statistical physics.
Claw-free graphs also have favorable algorithmic properties as several hard problems (e.g. maximum independent set and approximating the independence polynomial) can be solved in polynomial time in claw-free graphs
[
51
,
56
]
. The survey
[
29
]
describes many other properties of claw-free graphs and the areas in which they appear.

1.3.
Overview of Proofs

The solutions to the variational problems
\phi
and
\psi
are presented in
Section

3
. The proofs combine a version of the regularity lemma (
Lemma

2.4
) with the solution to an extremal problem over edge colorings (
Section

8
). Every optimizer
W
in the variational problem
\phi
is the limit of a sequence of regular partitions of claw-free graphs. The solution to the extremal problem allows us to deduce these regular partitions closely resemble (in a Hamming distance sense) the extremal structure, which can be informally described as a union of co-bipartite graphs and an empty graph. It then follows that
W
has the same structure subject to an edge density constraint, and examples of these graphons are depicted in
Figures

2
and
3
.

Theorem

1.5
is proven in
Sections

5
and
6
. The proofs use the Kleitman–Rothschild method, a technique for asymptotic enumeration that was first applied to the enumeration of partially-ordered sets
[
39
]
. In this paper, the method works by defining several subsets of
{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
, showing that the graphs
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
not contained in those subsets have a special structure, and proving that the cardinality of those subsets is much smaller than that of
{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
. The first step is to define
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{far}}\,
, the set of all
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
that are far in cut metric from the set of optimal graphons
{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
for the variational problem
\phi(\gamma)
.
Proposition

3.8
proves that almost all
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
are contained in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{close}}:={\mathcal{C}}(n,m)\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{far}}
. In
Lemmas

5.1
and
6.1
we prove that every graph
G\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{close}}
is within
o(n^{2})
edit distance of a graph
G^{\prime}
that is the disjoint union of several co-bipartite graphs with parts denoted
\Pi_{i}=\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
,
i=1,\dots,\ell
, and a sparse graph. The graph
T(G)
with edge set
E(G)\,\Delta\,E(G^{\prime})
is called the
defect graph
since we think of its edges as defects in
G
.

The bulk of the proof of
Theorem

1.5
deals with classifying defect graphs according to various graph theoretic conditions and bounding the number of graphs
G
for which a given defect graph
T
is optimal. The key tool in these steps is the version of Janson’s inequality due to
[
65
]
(
Theorem

2.1
). The inequality is applied by first setting up a random graph
H
that is the disjoint union of random co-bipartite graphs on the parts
\Pi_{i}=\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
, and including the defect graph. We then define a class
{\mathscr{K}}
of subgraphs
C\subseteq K_{V}
isomorphic to
K_{1,3}
and uses Janson’s inequality to obtain an exponential tail bound for the probability
H
contains none of the induced copies in
{\mathscr{K}}
. The tail bounds are written independently in
Section

4
.

To prove
Theorem

1.5

3
we first fix an optimizer
W
for
\phi(\gamma)
that has more than one co-bipartite block (one can visualize this by inspecting
Figure

3
). A detailed analysis shows almost every
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
that is close in cut metric to
W
is in fact a disjoint union of several co-bipartite graphs (where the number of co-bipartite graphs is roughly given by the number of such blocks in
W
) and a sparse graph. If
W^{\ast}
is the optimizer with a single co-bipartite block (i.e. the leftmost graphon in
Figure

3
), then the graphs
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
close to
W^{\ast}
in cut metric have at least
cn
more vertices in the sparse part of the graph than those close to
W
, where
c>0
is a constant. We can then lower bound the number of claw-free graphs close to
W^{\ast}
as follows: for every
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
that is close to
W
, replace the co-bipartite subgraphs of
G
with a single co-bipartite graph similar in size to the co-bipartite block in
W^{\ast}
; this frees up a set
U
of at least
cn
vertices in
G
; finally, place a cubic claw-free graph on
U
. Since the main result of
[
50
]
implies the number of cubic claw-free graphs is
n^{\Theta(n)}
, we conclude there are at least
n^{\Theta(n)}
as many graphs
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
that are close to
W^{\ast}
in cut metric than that are close to
W
.

1.4.
Future Work

A natural direction for future research is to examine the typical structure of sparse claw-free graphs, which could give a more precise understanding of the sparse graph
G_{2}
in
Theorem

1.5

3
. The proof of
Theorem

1.5

3
lower-bounds the number of claw-free graphs close to the optimal graphon
W^{\ast}
using cubic claw-free graphs, so the following is a pertinent question.

Question 1.7
.

What are the asymptotics (of the logarithm) of
|{\mathcal{C}}(n,cn)|
for fixed
c>0
?

The use of an asymmetric container lemma, as done by
[
52
]
to study sparse induced-
C_{4}
-free graphs
[
52
]
, could help analyze sparse claw-free graphs.

Another direction for future research could examine the typical structure of fixed-density induced-
H
-free graphs for other graphs
H
, and analyze whether phase transitions occur in those cases as well.

Question 1.8
.

For graphs
H
with coloring number
5
5

5

The coloring number was defined by
[
58
]

[
58
]
and is the suitable replacement for chromatic number in the induced setting.
at least three, does the variational problem over induced-
H
-free graphons exhibit a phase transition in the edge density? Is there always a phase in which the optimizer is nonunique?

The proofs for typical structure in claw-free graphs suggest similar results could hold for the class of induced-
K_{1,r+1}
-free graphs for
r\geqslant 3
. To establish
1.9
, it may be useful to prove asymptotics for the number of
(r+1)
-regular induced-
K_{1,r+1}
-free graphs, similar to the cubic claw-free case.

Conjecture 1.9
.

For all
r\geqslant 3
, the class
{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
of induced-
K_{1,r+1}
-free graphs on
n
vertices and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
edges exhibits a phase transition in the edge density
\gamma\in(0,1)
. In one phase, almost every
G\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
is co-
r
-partite, and in the other phase, almost every
G\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
is the disjoint union of a co-
r
-partite graph and a sparse graph.

Further directions of research also include solving the lower-tail variational problem for claws, or the fixed (non-zero) claw-density variational problem, which are likely to require new techniques.

2.
Preliminaries

This section describes probabilistic tools and background on graph regularity and graph limits that we will need. Unless stated otherwise, graphs are labeled and simple on a vertex set
V
of size
n
.

2.1.
Notation

Let
{\mathcal{G}}(n)
denote the set of graphs on
V
. Let
{\mathcal{G}}:=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{G}}(n)
denote the set of all graphs. Let
{\mathcal{C}}(n)
denote the set of claw-free graphs on
n
vertices and let
{\mathcal{B}}(n)
denote the set of bipartite graphs on
n
vertices. Let
{\mathcal{G}}(n,m)
denote the set of graphs on
n
vertices with
m
edges, and define the sets
{\mathcal{C}}(n,m):={\mathcal{C}}(n)\cap{\mathcal{G}}(n,m)
and
{\mathcal{B}}(n,m):={\mathcal{B}}(n)\cap{\mathcal{G}}(n,m)
. For all
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
let
{\mathcal{U}}(n)
denote the set of unlabeled graphs on
n
vertices. Let
{\mathcal{U}}(n,m)
denote those unlabeled graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges. Let
{\mathcal{U}}:=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{U}}(n)
denote the set of all unlabeled graphs.

If
G
is a graph and
u,v\in V
are distinct vertices then
uv
denotes the unordered pair
\{u,v\}
. For a set
S
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\binom{S}{k}:=\{T\subseteq S:|T|=k\}
. We denote
v(G):=|V(G)|
and
e(G):=|E(G)|
. For all
A,B\subseteq V(G)
, denote
E(A):=E(G)\cap\binom{A}{2}
,
e(A):=|E(A)|
,
E(A,B):=\{uv\in E(G):u\in A,v\in B\}
, and
e(A,B):=|E(A,B)|
. The
density
between
A
and
B
is the number
d(A,B):=e(A,B)/(|A|\cdot|B|)
. For all
v\in V(G)
, the neighborhood (resp. degree) of
v
in
A
is defined by
N(v,A):=N(v)\cap A
(resp.
d(v,A):=|N(v,A)|
). The complementary neighborhood (resp. degree) of
v
in
A
is defined by
\overline{N}(v,A):=A\setminus N(v)
(resp.
\overline{d}(v,A):=|\overline{N}(v,A)|
). Define
G[A,B]
to be the graph with edge set
E=\{ab\in E(G):a\in A,\,b\in B\}
and vertex set
\bigcup E
. For each of the previous definitions, we sometimes use subscripts (e.g.
E_{G}(A)
,
e_{G}(A)
,
d_{G}(A,B)
, etc) to emphasize the underlying graph. The complement of a graph
G
is denoted
\overline{G}
or
G^{c}
. For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, denote
[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}
. For functions
f,g:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the formula
f(n)\ll g(n)
means
\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)/g(n)=0
.

2.2.
Probabilistic Preliminaries

In
Section

4
we use the following version of Janson’s inequality due to
[
65
]

[
65
]
to prove exponential penalties for defect edges. The reason we need this general version is that the event of containing an induced
K_{1,3}
is not edge-monotone, but if the copies of
K_{1,3}
are chosen judiciously then the events of containing those copies satisfy the general up-set conditions.

Theorem 2.1

(Janson’s inequality,
[
65
]
)
.

Let
(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},{\mathbb{P}})
be a probability space. Let
{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}
be a family of events such that for all
A,B\in{\mathcal{I}}
we have
{\mathbb{P}}\{A\cap B\}\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}\{A\}{\mathbb{P}}\{B\}
,
A\cap B\in{\mathcal{I}}
, and
A\cup B\in{\mathcal{I}}
. Let
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\in{\mathcal{I}}
and define

\mu:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{P}}\{B_{i}\}\hskip 19.91692pt\text{and}\hskip 19.91692pt\Delta:=\sum_{i\sim j}{\mathbb{P}}\{B_{i}\wedge B_{j}\}\,,

where the second sum is over unordered pairs
i
,
j
such that
B_{i}
,
B_{j}
are not independent. Then

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\overline{B_{i}}\right\}\leqslant\exp\left(-\min\left\{\frac{\mu}{2}\,,\,\frac{\mu^{2}}{4\Delta}\right\}\right)\,.

2.3.
Preliminaries on Graph Regularity

Let
G
be a graph and
A,B\subseteq V(G)
. The pair
\{A,B\}
is said to be
\epsilon
-regular
if for all subsets
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
with
|A^{\prime}|\geqslant\epsilon|A|
and
|B^{\prime}|\geqslant\epsilon|B|
, we have
|d(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})-d(A,B)|\leqslant\epsilon
. For
\epsilon>0
, a partition
\{V_{0},\dots,V_{k}\}
of
V(G)
(where
V_{0}
is possibly empty) is said to be an
\epsilon
-regular partition
of
G
if the following conditions hold:

(1)

|V_{0}|\leqslant\epsilon|V(G)|
,

(2)

|V_{i}|=|V_{j}|
for all
i,j\in[k]
,

(3)

all but at most
\epsilon k^{2}
pairs
\{V_{i},V_{j}\}
,
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k
, are
\epsilon
-regular.

The sets
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}
are called the
clusters
or
parts
and
V_{0}
the
exceptional set
of the
\epsilon
-regular partition.

In our proofs we will need regular partitions equipped with additional information, namely labels on clusters and pairs of clusters. When applying induced embedding lemmas, these labels describe whether edges, non-edges, or both edges and non-edges of a fixed graph
H
can be embedded in a given cluster or between a pair of clusters. These kinds of regular partitions have appeared in several past works including
[
3
,
13
,
4
,
49
,
16
]
. We will use the definitions and embedding lemmas from
[
16
]
, which we record now.

For a graph
G
and constants
\mu>0
,
\epsilon>0
, and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a
(\mu,\epsilon,k)
-subpartition
of
G
is a set of pairwise disjoint subsets
W_{1},\dots,W_{k}\subseteq V(G)
such that
|W_{i}|\geqslant\mu|V(G)|
for all
i\in[k]
, and every pair
\{W_{i},W_{j}\}
with
i\neq j
is
\epsilon
-regular. A
(\mu,\epsilon,k)
-subpartition of a graph
G
is said to be
dense
if every pair
\{W_{i},W_{j}\}
of distinct subsets has
d(W_{i},W_{j})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
, and
sparse
if every pair
\{W_{i},W_{j}\}
of distinct subsets has
d(W_{i},W_{j})<\frac{1}{2}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

(
[
3
, Corollary 3.4]
)

For all
\epsilon>0
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
there exists
\mu=\mu(\epsilon,k)>0
such that every graph on at least
\mu^{-1}
vertices has a dense or sparse
(\mu,\epsilon,k)
-subpartition.

Definition 2.3

(Type coloring and type of a graph)
.

A
type coloring
of a graph
R=(V,E)
is a mapping
\sigma:V\cup E\to\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\}
such that
\sigma(V)\subseteq\{0,1\}
. Let
G
be a graph and
V(G)=V_{0}\cup V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup V_{k}
an
\epsilon
-regular partition of
G
. Let
V_{R}:=[k]
and
E_{R}:=\{ij:\{V_{i},V_{j}\}\text{ is }\epsilon\text{-regular}\}
. For all constants
\delta>0
,
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, and
k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, an
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\delta,k^{\prime})
-type
associated with the
\epsilon
-regular partition
V(G)=V_{0}\cup V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup V_{k}
is a type-colored graph
R=(V_{R},E_{R},\sigma)
, where
\sigma:V_{R}\cup E_{R}\to\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\}
is a mapping such that for all
ij\in E_{R}
,

\sigma(ij)=\begin{cases}0&d(V_{i},V_{j})\leqslant\delta\\
\frac{1}{2}&\delta<d(V_{i},V_{j})<1-\delta\\
1&d(V_{i},V_{j})\geqslant 1-\delta\end{cases}

and for all
i\in V_{R}
,

\sigma(i)=\begin{cases}0&G[V_{i}]\text{ has a sparse }(\mu,\epsilon^{\prime},k^{\prime})\text{-subpartition}\\
1&G[V_{i}]\text{ has a dense }(\mu,\epsilon^{\prime},k^{\prime})\text{-subpartition}\end{cases}\,,

where
\mu=\mu(\epsilon^{\prime},k^{\prime})
is the constant from
Lemma

2.2
. A type
R^{\prime}
is said to
refine
a type
R
if the partition
P^{\prime}
associated to
R^{\prime}
is a refinement of the partition associated to
R
, that is, for all
S^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}
there exists
S\in P
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
.

We refer to the three types of edges
\sigma(ij)=0
,
\frac{1}{2}
, and
1
as
sparse
,
random-like
, and
dense
, respectively. Similarly, we refer to the two types of edges
\sigma(i)=0
and
1
as
sparse
and
dense
, respectively.

The following version of the regularity lemma asserts that every sufficiently large graph has a type.

Lemma 2.4

(Type Lemma,
[
16
, Lemma 2.7]
)
.

For all
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}>0
and all
l,k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist positive integers
u
and
n_{0}
such that for all
\delta>0
, every graph
G
on at least
n_{0}
vertices has an
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\delta,k^{\prime})
-type
R=(V_{R},E_{R},\sigma)
with
l\leqslant|V_{R}|\leqslant u
.

Let
H=(V_{H},E_{H})
be a graph and
R=(V_{R},E_{R},\sigma)
a type-colored graph. A
colored homomorphism
from
H
to
R
is a mapping
\phi:V_{H}\to V_{R}
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

For all distinct
u,v\in V_{H}
, if
\phi(u)\neq\phi(v)
then
\phi(u)\phi(v)\in E_{R}
.

(2)

If
uv\in E_{H}
then either
\phi(u)\neq\phi(v)
and
\sigma(\phi(u)\phi(v))\in\{\frac{1}{2},1\}
, or
\phi(u)=\phi(v)
and
\sigma(\phi(u))=1
.

(3)

If
uv\not\in E_{H}
then either
\phi(u)\neq\phi(v)
and
\sigma(\phi(u)\phi(v))\in\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}
, or
\phi(u)=\phi(v)
and
\sigma(\phi(u))=0
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(Induced Embedding Lemma,
[
16
, Lemma 2.9]
)

For all
k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and all
\delta>0
there exist
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}>0
such that the following holds. Let
G
be a graph with an
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\delta,k^{\prime})
-type
R
. If
H
is a graph on
v(H)\leqslant k^{\prime}
vertices and there exists a colored homomorphism from
H
to
R
, then
G
contains
H
as an induced subgraph.

The following is a simple consequence of
Lemma

2.5
stating that if a type
R
of a graph
G
has certain type-colored triangles (namely, three random-like edges, or two random-like edges and one sparse edge), then
G
necessarily contains
K_{1,3}
as an induced subgraph.

Lemma 2.6
.

For all
k^{\prime}\geqslant 4
and
\delta>0
, there exist constants
\epsilon_{0},\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}>0
such that the following holds for all
0<\epsilon\leqslant\epsilon_{0}
and
0<\epsilon^{\prime}\leqslant\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}
. Let
G
be a graph with an
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\delta,k^{\prime})
-type
R=(V_{R},E_{R},\sigma)
. Suppose there exist distinct vertices
u,v,w\in V(R)
such that either (or both) of the following cases hold: (
i
)
\sigma(uv)=\sigma(vw)=\sigma(wu)=\frac{1}{2}
; or (
ii
)
\sigma(uv)=\sigma(uw)=\frac{1}{2}
and
\sigma(vw)=0
. Then
G
contains
K_{1,3}
as an induced subgraph.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{\ref{lemma:InducedEmbedding}}>0
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}=\epsilon^{\prime}_{\ref{lemma:InducedEmbedding}}>0
be the constants provided when
Lemma

2.5
is applied with
k^{\prime}
and
\delta
. Let
0<\epsilon\leqslant\epsilon_{0}
and
0<\epsilon^{\prime}\leqslant\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}
and assume
R=(V_{R},E_{R},\sigma)
is an
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\delta,k^{\prime})
-type for
G
satisfying the hypotheses of the lemma. By
Lemma

2.5
, it suffices to prove there exists a colored homomorphism from
K_{1,3}
to
R
. Let
V(K_{1,3})=\{a,b,c,d\}
be a labeling of the vertices of
K_{1,3}
where
a
is the vertex of degree three. First, if
\sigma(u)=0
, then in both cases (
i
) and (
ii
), the mapping given by
a\mapsto v
and
b,c,d\mapsto u
is a colored homomorphism. Otherwise we have
\sigma(u)=1
, and the mapping given by
a,b\mapsto u
,
c\mapsto v
, and
d\mapsto w
is a colored homomorphism.
∎

2.4.
Preliminaries on Graph Limits and the Cut Metric

We record some facts about graph limits that we will need. See
[
41
]
for a general reference on the subject.

Let
F
and
G
be graphs. Let
\hom(F,G)
denote the set of homomorphisms from
F
to
G
, that is, maps
\phi:V(F)\to V(G)
such that
uv\in E(F)
implies
\phi(uv)\in E(G)
. Let
\homind(F,G)
denote the set of induced homomorphisms from
F
to
G
, that is, maps
\phi:V(F)\to V(G)
such that
uv\in E(F)
if and only if
\phi(uv)\in E(G)
. The
homomorphism density
of
F
in
G
is defined by

t(F,G):=\frac{|\hom(F,G)|}{v(G)^{v(F)}}\,,

and, assuming
v(F)\leqslant v(G)
, the
induced homomorphism density
of
F
in
G
is defined by

t_{\ind}(F,G):=\frac{|\homind(F,G)|}{v(G)(v(G)-1)\cdots(v(G)-v(F)+1)}\,.

The definitions of
t
and
t_{\ind}
make sense for unlabeled graphs by fixing an arbitrary labeling of the vertex sets.

A sequence
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of graphs is said to be
convergent
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}t(F,G_{n})
exists for all fixed graphs
F
. We obtain an equivalence relation on the set of convergent graph sequences by considering two sequences equivalent if and only if they have the same limiting homomorphism densities for all graphs
F
. For all subsets
{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
of unlabeled graphs, let
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}
denote the set of equivalence classes
\Gamma\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}^{\mathbb{N}}
of convergent graph sequences such that there exists a graph sequence
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathbb{N}}\cap\Gamma
. The set
\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}
is a completion of
{\mathcal{U}}
, where
{\mathcal{U}}
is isometrically embedded in
\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}
with respect to the cut metric (defined below). For all subsets
{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
denote
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}:=\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}\setminus{\mathcal{V}}
, and call the elements of
\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}

graph limits
.

If
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\Gamma\in\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
then write
G_{n}\to\Gamma
and define, for all graphs
F
,

t(F,\Gamma):=\lim_{n\to\infty}t(F,G_{n})\,.

Let
{\mathcal{W}}
denote the set of symmetric measurable functions
W:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1]
(symmetric here means
W(x,y)=W(y,x)
). The elements of
{\mathcal{W}}
are called
graphons
. For all
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
and all graphs
F
, define the
homomorphism density
of
F
in
W

t(F,W):=\int_{[0,1]^{v(f)}}\prod_{uv\in E(F)}W(x_{u},x_{v})\prod_{u\in V(F)}dx_{u}\,,

and the
induced homomorphism density
of
F
in
W

t_{\ind}(F,W):=\int_{[0,1]^{v(F)}}\prod_{uv\in E(F)}W(x_{u},x_{v})\prod_{uv\in E(F^{c})}(1-W(x_{u},x_{v}))\prod_{u\in V(F)}dx_{u}\,.

[
42
]
proved in
[
42
]
that for all graph limits
\Gamma
there exists a graphon
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
such that
t(F,W)=t(F,\Gamma)
for all graphs
F
, and conversely for all
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
there is a unique graph limit
\Gamma
such that
t(F,W)=t(F,\Gamma)
for all
F
. We say that the graphon
W

represents

\Gamma
and write
\widehat{W}:=\Gamma
. Similarly if
{\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq{\mathcal{W}}
then we denote
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}:=\{\widehat{W}:W\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}
. Two graphons are
equivalent
if they represent the same graph limit; see
Footnote

4
for a characterization of equivalent graphons. If
W
is a graphon representing
\Gamma
and
G_{n}\to\Gamma
then we also write
G_{n}\to W
.

On several occasions we will work with graphons that are naturally associated to matrices, graphs, and regular partitions. For a matrix
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
let
W_{A}\in{\mathcal{W}}
denote the graphon defined by
W_{A}(x,y)=A_{\lceil nx\rceil,\lceil ny\rceil}
for
\min\{x,y\}>0
and
W_{A}(x,y)=0
for
\min\{x,y\}=0
. For all graphs
G\in{\mathcal{G}}(n)
let
A_{G}
denote the adjacency matrix of
G
with all ones on the diagonal, and if
G
is a weighted graph then let
A_{G}
denote the weights matrix of
G
. For all (weighted or unweighted) graphs
G
, denote
W_{G}:=W_{A_{G}}
. Let
R
be an
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\delta,k^{\prime})
-type associated to an
\epsilon
-regular partition
V(G)=V_{0}\cup V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup V_{k}
of
G
(as in
Definition

2.3
). The
density matrix
of
R
is defined to be the
k\times k
matrix
Q
with
Q_{ij}=d(V_{i},V_{j})
. The graphon
W_{R}\in{\mathcal{W}}
is defined as follows: let
a:=|V_{0}|/n
and let
b:=|V_{1}|/n
; for all
x\in[0,1]
let
i_{x}=0
if
x\in[0,a]
or
\left\lceil\frac{x-a}{b}\right\rceil
if
x\in(a,1]
; let
W_{R}(x,y)=d(V_{i_{x}},V_{i_{y}})
. For all graph limits
\Gamma\in\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
let
W_{\Gamma}\in{\mathcal{W}}
be a canonically chosen graphon that represents
\Gamma
.

The
cut norm
of a graphon
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
is defined to be

\lVert W\rVert_{\square}:=\sup_{S,T\subseteq[0,1]}\left|\int_{S\times T}W(x,y)\,dx\,dy\right|\,.

For all
W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}
define

\delta_{\square}(W,W^{\prime}):=\inf_{\phi}\lVert W-W^{\prime}\circ(\phi\otimes\phi)\rVert_{\square}\,,

where the infimum is over all measure-preserving bijections
\phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
. Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{w}
denote the set of weighted graphs with weights in
[0,1]
, and we view
{\mathcal{G}}
as being a subset of
{\mathcal{G}}_{w}
. For all
\Gamma\in\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}\cup{\mathcal{G}}_{w}\cup{\mathcal{W}}
, define
K(\Gamma)
to be the graphon
W_{\Gamma}
if
\Gamma\in\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}\cup{\mathcal{G}}_{w}
, and define
K(\Gamma):=\Gamma
if
\Gamma\in{\mathcal{W}}
. The
cut metric

\delta_{\square}
is then defined on pairs
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in(\overline{{\mathcal{U}}}\cup{\mathcal{G}}_{w}\cup{\mathcal{W}})^{2}
by

(4)

\delta_{\square}(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}):=\delta_{\square}(K(\Gamma_{1}),K(\Gamma_{2}))\,.

The
cut norm
of a real
n\times n
matrix
A
is defined to be

\lVert A\rVert_{\square}:=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\max_{S,T\subseteq[n]}\left|\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}A_{ij}\right|\,.

The
cut distance
between two weighted graphs
G,G^{\prime}
on
n
vertices is defined as

d_{\square}(G,G^{\prime}):=\lVert A_{G}-A_{G^{\prime}}\rVert_{\square}\,,

and one also defines the distance

\widehat{\delta}_{\square}(G,G^{\prime}):=\min_{P}\lVert A_{G}-P^{T}A_{G^{\prime}}P\rVert_{\square}\,,

where the minimum is over
n\times n
permutation matrices
P
.

The following lemma, almost immediate from the proof of
[
42
, Theorem 2.2]
is a key preliminary step in our solution to the graphon variational problem since it provides, for a given claw-free graphon, a sequence of types of claw-free graphs.

Lemma 2.7
.

For all
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
such that
t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W)=0
and all
\delta>0
, there exists a sequence
\{W_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of graphons such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

\lVert W_{m}-W\rVert_{1}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
.

(2)

For all
m
there exist constants
\eta_{m},\eta^{\prime}_{m}>0
and
k^{\prime}\geqslant 4
such that
W_{m}
is the graphon associated with an
(\eta_{m},\eta^{\prime}_{m},\delta/5,k^{\prime})
-type
R_{m}
of an claw-free graph. Additionally, the constants
\eta_{m},\eta^{\prime}_{m},\delta/5,k^{\prime}
satisfy the hypotheses of
Lemma

2.5
for all
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
.

(3)

\eta_{m}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
.

(4)

The number of clusters of
R_{m}
diverges as
m\to\infty
.

Definition 2.8

(
W
-random graph)
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
, let
G(n,W)
be the random graph on the vertex set
[n]
sampled as follows. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
and
Y_{ij}
,
\{i,j\}\in\binom{[n]}{2}
, be i.i.d. uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
. For all
\{i,j\}\in\binom{[n]}{2}
, form an edge between vertices
i
and
j
if and only if
Y_{ij}\leqslant W(X_{i},X_{j})
. If
\Gamma
is a graph limit then let
G(n,\Gamma)
denote the random graph
G(n,W)
for any graphon
W
representing
\Gamma
(and note the distribution of
G(n,\Gamma)
is independent of
W
).

Proof of
Lemma

2.7
.

The sequence of random graphs
G_{n}:=G(n,W)
converges to
W
in cut metric with probability 1 (see
[
42
, Theorem 2.5]
), and each
G_{n}
is claw-free almost surely. Let
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}
be the constants given when
Lemma

2.5
is applied with
k^{\prime}
and
\delta
. By applying the type lemma (
Lemma

2.4
) instead of weak regularity in the proof of
[
42
, Theorem 2.2]
, we obtain a subsequence
\{G^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
\{G_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that the following conditions hold. First, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
j\in[n]
, the graph
G^{\prime}_{n}
has an
(\eta,\epsilon^{\prime},\delta,k^{\prime})
-type
R_{n,j}
, where
\eta:=\min\{\epsilon,1/j\}
, such that
R_{n,j}
has
k_{j}
clusters and
k_{j}\times k_{j}
density matrix
Q_{n,j}
. Second, for all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a
k_{j}\times k_{j}
matrix
Q_{j}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}Q_{n,j}\to Q_{j}
. Finally, letting
W_{j}:=W_{Q_{j}}\in{\mathcal{W}}
denote the natural graphon associated with
Q_{j}
, we have
W_{j}\to W
almost everywhere, hence
\lVert W_{m}-W\rVert_{1}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
.
∎

2.5.
Preliminaries on the Entropy of Graph Limits

For all graphons
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
, the entropy of
W
is defined to be

H(W):=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}H(W(x,y))\,dx\,dy\,.

The entropy of a graph limit
\Gamma\in\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
is defined to be
H(\Gamma):=H(W)
for any
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
representing
W
(and this is independent of
W
). For all
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
and
p\in(0,1)
the relative entropy of
W
is defined by

I_{p}(W):=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}I_{p}(W(x,y))\,dx\,dy\,,

where
I_{p}
is the function defined in
(
3
)
. The relative entropy of a graph limit
\Gamma\in\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
is defined by
I_{p}(\Gamma):=I_{p}(W)
for any
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
representing
\Gamma
. For a graphon
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
define the quantity

\rand(W):=|\{(x,y):0<W(x,y)<1\}|\,,

where
|\cdot|
denote Lebesgue measure. For a graph limit
\Gamma\in\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
let
\rand(\Gamma):=\rand(W)
for any
W
representing
\Gamma
.

If
{\mathcal{Q}}
is a set of graphs and
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
then let
{\mathcal{Q}}(n):=\{G\in{\mathcal{Q}}:v(G)=n\}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}(n,m):=\{G\in{\mathcal{Q}}(n):e(G)=m\}
. The following lemma is a simple consequence of
[
31
, Theorem 1]
, proven in
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 2.9
.

If
{\mathcal{Q}}\neq\emptyset
is a set of graphs,
p\in(0,1)
is a constant, and
G\sim G(n,p)
then

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{Q}}(n)\}\leqslant\sup_{\Gamma\in\overline{{\mathcal{Q}}}}I_{p}(\Gamma)\,.

If
H
is a fixed graph and
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
then let
{\mathcal{F}}(n,H)
denote the set of induced-
H
-free graphs on
n
vertices, and let
{\mathcal{F}}(n,m,H):={\mathcal{F}}(n,H)\cap{\mathcal{G}}(n,m)
. The following propositions, proven in
Appendix

B
, state that the rate function and entropy density for induced-
H
-free graphs is given by a variational problem over a set of induced-
H
-free graphons.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
p\in(0,1)
be a constant and
G\sim G(n,p)
. If
H
is a fixed graph and

{\mathcal{F}}:=\{W\in{\mathcal{W}}:t_{\ind}(H,W)=0,\,\rand(W)>0\}

is nonempty then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,H)\}=\sup_{W\in{\mathcal{F}}}I_{p}(W)\,.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. If
H
is a fixed graph and

{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}:=\{W\in{\mathcal{W}}:t(K_{2},W)=\gamma,\,t_{\ind}(H,W)=0,\,\rand(W)>0\}

is nonempty then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}|{\mathcal{F}}(n,m,H)|=\sup_{W\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}}H(W)\,.

3.
Solving the Graphon Variational Problem

In this section we solve the variational problem over claw-free graphons with edge density
\gamma
. Several definitions, notations, and lemmas used in this section were recorded in
Sections

2.4
and
2.5
. For all
\gamma\in(0,1)
, define the set of graphons

(5)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}:=\big\{W\in{\mathcal{W}}:t(K_{2},W)=\gamma\,,\,t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W)=0\big\}\,,

and the variational problem

(6)

\phi(\gamma):=\sup\{H(W):W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}\}\,.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}^{\ast}
denote the set of all
W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
that achieve the supremum in
(
6
)
. Note
{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
is nonempty since
{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
is compact (see
[
15
, Theorem 3.7]
) and
H
is upper semicontinuous (see
[
18
, Lemma 2.1]
).

3.1.
Overview of Proof

The solution to the variational problem
(
6
)
uses types (introduced in
Section

2
) and an extremal problem over edge colorings (
Lemmas

3.2
and
3.3
).
Lemma

2.6
shows that if
R
is a type for a claw-free graph, then the type coloring of
R
avoids certain substructures:
R
contains no triangle with three random-like edges, nor any triangle with two random-like edges and one sparse edge.
Lemma

3.2
proves that a 3-edge-coloring avoiding these substructures has at most as many random-like edges as dense edges, and
Proposition

3.3
proves extremal structure and stability. We use these results to solve
(
6
)
as follows.

Every optimal graphon
W
for
\phi
is the limit of a sequence
R_{n}
of types associated with claw-free graphs on
n
vertices. The concavity of
I_{p}
implies that
W
takes three values
\{0,a,1\}
almost everywhere for some
a\in(0,1)
. The three values of
W
approximately correspond with the three types of edge labels in
R_{n}
. Since
\int W=\gamma
, we have
\gamma=x+ay
and
H(W)=yH(a)
, where
x
is the measure of the set where
W=1
and
y
is the measure of the set where
W=a
. Since
R_{n}
has at most as many random-like edges as dense edges, we show that
y\leqslant x
. Further,
yH(a)
is maximum if and only if
x=y
, and there is a threshold at
\gamma^{\ast}=\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}
: for all
\gamma\geqslant\gamma^{\ast}
, we have
x=y=\frac{1}{2}
while for
\gamma<\gamma^{\ast}
, we have
x=y=\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{20}\gamma
. For large
n
, the equality
x=y
means the number of random-like edges in
R_{n}
is very close to the number of dense edges. The stability result
Proposition

3.3
then implies
R_{n}
is closed in Hamming distance to a certain extremal structure and allows us to deduce
W
is equivalent to a graphon in a set
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
of well-structured graphons defined below.

For all
\gamma\geqslant\gamma^{\ast}
, the set
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
contains one element (up to equivalence), the unique optimal graphon at edge density
\gamma
. For
\gamma<\gamma^{\ast}
, the set
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
has infinitely many (nonequivalent) graphons. Since every graphon in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
achieves the same entropy, has edge density
\gamma
, and is claw-free, we prove
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
is precisely the set of optimizers for
\phi
.

We now define the graphons that we will prove comprise
{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
. Let
\xi:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to[0,1]
be the step function defined by

\xi(x,y)=\begin{cases}1&(x,y)\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)^{2}\cup\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)^{2}\\[3.0pt]
\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}&(x,y)\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)\times\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\cup\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\times\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)\\[3.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Let
\Lambda
denote the set of all sequences
(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots)
of at least two real numbers (the sequence may be finite or infinite) such that three conditions hold: (
i
)
\lambda_{0}=0
, (
ii
)
\lambda_{i}<\lambda_{i+1}\leqslant 1
, and (
iii
) the numbers
\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}
are nonincreasing in
i
. For all
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots)\in\Lambda
, define the graphon
W_{\bm{\lambda}}\in{\mathcal{W}}
by

(7)

W_{\bm{\lambda}}(x,y)=\sum_{0\leqslant i<|\bm{\lambda}|}\xi\left(\frac{x-\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}},\frac{y-\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}}\right)\,,

where
|\bm{\lambda}|
denotes the cardinality of
\bm{\lambda}
as a set. (
Figures

2
and
3
depict such graphons
W_{\bm{\lambda}}
.) Let
{\mathcal{V}}:=\{W_{\bm{\lambda}}:\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda\}
denote the set of all such graphons. For all
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle:=\left\{W_{\bm{\lambda}}:\int W_{\bm{\lambda}}(x,y)\,dx\,dy=\gamma\,,\,\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{W_{\bm{\lambda}}:\sum_{0\leqslant i<|\bm{\lambda}|}(\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i})^{2}=\gamma\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}\right)\,,\,\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0}\,,\,\lambda_{1}\,,\,\dots)\in\Lambda\right\}\,,

and for all
\gamma\in\big[\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big)
let
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
be the set containing the single graphon
W^{\ast}_{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
defined by

(8)

W^{\ast}_{\gamma}(x,y)=\begin{cases}1&(x,y)\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)^{2}\cup\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)^{2}\\[3.0pt]
2\gamma-1&(x,y)\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)\times\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\cup\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\times\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)\\[2.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Notice that for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
we have
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
. Also note that for all
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
, there are graphons
W_{\bm{\lambda}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
for which
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots)
is an infinite sequence (in fact, there are infinitely many non-equivalent such graphons). For example, for edge density
\gamma=\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{12}
, the set
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
contains the graphon
W_{\bm{\lambda}}
where
\bm{\lambda}=(0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4},\frac{7}{8},\dots)
.

The following proposition states the solution to the variational problem
(
6
)
. In the statement, recall that if
{\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq{\mathcal{W}}
is a set of graphons then
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
denotes the set of graph limits represented by elements of
{\mathcal{Z}}
. This notation allows us to state that two sets of graphons are the same up to equivalence (in the sense of
Footnote

4
).

Proposition 3.1
.

For all
\gamma\in(0,1)
we have
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{\gamma}
.

The proof of
Proposition

3.1
uses extremal and stability properties of edge colorings, which we state now and prove in
Section

8
. If
\varphi
is a red–green–blue coloring of
E(K_{n})
, let
e_{r}(\varphi)
,
e_{g}(\varphi)
, and
e_{b}(\varphi)
denote the number of red, green, and blue edges of
\varphi
, respectively.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\varphi
be a red–green–blue coloring of
E(K_{n})
. If
\varphi
has no triangle colored (red, red, red) nor any colored (red, red, green) then

(9)

e_{r}(\varphi)\leqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor\,.

The next proposition gives a structural characterization of the colorings
\varphi
for which
(
9
)
is close to equality. We introduce the following definitions to make the extremal structure precise. Let
{\mathcal{E}}(n)
be the set of red–green–blue colorings
\varphi
of
E(K_{n})
such that for some partition of the vertex set
V(K_{n})=H_{1}\cup\cdots\cup H_{l}
, all of the following conditions hold: if
n
is odd then exactly one of the numbers
|H_{i}|
is odd, otherwise all
|H_{i}|
are even; for all
i\in[l]
there is a balanced partition
V(H_{i})=X_{i}\cup Y_{i}
(i.e. the sizes of
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
differ by at most one) such that the edges
E(X_{i})\cup E(Y_{i})
are all blue and the edges
E(X_{i},Y_{i})
are all red; for all
i\neq j
the edges
E(H_{i},H_{j})
are all green. The
Hamming distance
between colorings
\varphi
and
\psi
of
E(K_{n})
is

d(\varphi,\psi):=|\{e\in E(K_{n}):\varphi(e)\neq\psi(e)\}|\,.

Suppose
\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}(k)
,
k\leqslant n
, and
\sigma:V(K_{k})\to V(K_{n})
is injective. Let
\varphi_{\sigma}
be the red–green–blue coloring of
E(K_{n})
defined by

\varphi_{\sigma}(e)=\begin{cases}\varphi(\sigma^{-1}(e))&e\subseteq\image(\sigma)\\
\text{green}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,,

and define the set

(10)

{\mathcal{F}}(n):=\big\{\varphi_{\sigma}:k\in[n],\,\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}(k),\,\sigma:V(K_{k})\to V(K_{n})\text{ injective}\big\}\,.

The Hamming distance from
\varphi
to
{\mathcal{F}}(n)
is defined by

d(\varphi,{\mathcal{F}}(n)):=\min\{d(\varphi,\psi):\psi\in{\mathcal{F}}(n)\}\,,

so
d(\varphi,{\mathcal{F}}(n))
is the least number of edits one can make to the colors of
\varphi
to obtain a member of
{\mathcal{F}}(n)
.

Proposition 3.3
.

For all
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that the following holds. Let
\varphi
be a red–green–blue coloring of
E(K_{n})
. Assume
\varphi
has no triangle colored (red, red, red) nor any colored (red, red, green). If

e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor-\delta n^{2}

then
d(\varphi,{\mathcal{F}}(n))\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}
.

We will now state and prove several more lemmas in preparation for the proof of
Proposition

3.1
. For all
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
define the following subsets of
[0,1]^{2}
:

\displaystyle R_{W}

\displaystyle:=\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^{2}:0<W(x,y)<1\}\,,

\displaystyle O_{W}

\displaystyle:=\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^{2}:W(x,y)=1\}\,.

For a measurable set
A\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, the symbol
|A|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
A
.

Lemma 3.4
.

If
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
and
t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W)=0
then
|R_{W}|\leqslant|O_{W}|
.

Proof.

Fix
\delta>0
and let
\{W_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be the sequence of graphons obtained by applying
Lemma

2.7
with
W
and
\delta
. Then each
W_{m}
is the graphon associated with an
(\eta_{m},\eta_{m}^{\prime},\delta/5,k^{\prime})
-type
R=(V_{R},E_{R},\sigma)
of a claw-free graph
G
, where the constants
\eta_{m}
,
\eta_{m}^{\prime}
,
\delta/5
,
k^{\prime}
satisfy the hypotheses of the induced embedding lemma (
Lemma

2.5
). Since
G
is claw-free,
Lemma

2.6
implies
R
does not have three distinct vertices
u,v,w\in V_{R}
such that either of the following cases holds: (
i
)
\sigma(uv)=\sigma(vw)=\sigma(wu)=\frac{1}{2}
; or (
ii
)
\sigma(uv)=\sigma(uw)=\frac{1}{2}
and
\sigma(vw)=0
. Let
k:=|V_{R}|
and define a red–green–blue coloring
\varphi
of
E(K_{k})
as follows: an edge
uv\in E(K_{k})
is red if
\sigma(ij)=\frac{1}{2}
; green if
\sigma(uv)=0
; and blue if
\sigma(uv)=1
or
uv\not\in E_{R}
. It follows that
\varphi
has no triangle colored (red, red, red) nor any colored (red, red, green), hence
Lemma

3.2
implies
e_{r}(\varphi)\leqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
, where
e_{r}(\varphi)
and
e_{b}(\varphi)
denote the number of red and blue edges, respectively. Since
R
has at most
\eta_{m}k^{2}
non-edges,

|\{(x,y):\delta<W_{m}(x,y)<1-\delta\}|\leqslant|\{(x,y):W_{m}(x,y)\geqslant 1-\delta\}|+\eta_{m}+\frac{2}{k}\,.

Since
W_{m}\to W
in
L^{1}
,
\eta_{m}\to 0
, and
\delta>0
was arbitrary, it follows that
|R_{W}|\leqslant|O_{W}|
.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

If
W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}^{\ast}
then for some
a\in(0,1)
,
W
takes the values
\{0,a,1\}
almost everywhere.

Proof.

Fix
W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}^{\ast}
and let
R:=R_{W}
. Note that
R
has positive Lebesgue measure since otherwise
H(W)=0
. Define the graphon
W^{\prime}
by

W^{\prime}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{|R|}\int_{R}W(x,y)\,dx\,dy&(x,y)\in R\\
W(x,y)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

Notice that
W^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
since the relations
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
and
t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W)=0
imply
t(K_{2},W^{\prime})=\gamma
and
t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W^{\prime})=0
. The inequality
H(W)\geqslant H(W^{\prime})
holds by assumption. The concavity of
H
implies

H(W)=\int_{R}H(W(x,y))\,dx\,dy\,\leqslant|R|\cdot H\left(\frac{1}{|R|}\int_{R}W(x,y)\,dx\,dy\right)=H(W^{\prime})\,,

with equality holding if and only if
W
is constant almost everywhere in
R
.
∎

The following lemma is proven in
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Fix
c\in(0,1)
. Let
D\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
be the domain of points
(x,y)
such that
0<y\leqslant x\leqslant 1-y
and
0\leqslant\frac{c-x}{y}\leqslant 1
. Let
f:D\to{\mathbb{R}}
be defined by
f(x,y)=yH(\frac{c-x}{y})
. The function
f
attains a unique maximum
(x^{\ast},y^{\ast})
, given in terms of
c
by

(11)

x^{\ast}=y^{\ast}=\begin{cases}\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{10}c\phantom{...}&c\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)\\[5.0pt]
\frac{1}{2}&c\in\big[\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big)\end{cases}\,.

Additionally, the mapping
c\mapsto f(x^{\ast},y^{\ast})
has a continuous first derivative.

Lemma 3.7
.

For all
\gamma\in(0,1)
we have
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{\gamma}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{\gamma}^{\ast}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}^{\ast}
. We will establish an upper bound on
\phi(\gamma)
and observe that all
W\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
meet this upper bound. By
Lemma

3.5
we may restrict the domain of the variational problem
(
6
)
to the set
{\mathcal{Y}}
of all
W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
that take three values almost everywhere. Notice that
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{Y}}
. Let
W\in{\mathcal{Y}}
and assume
W
takes the values
\{0,a,1\}
almost everywhere for some
a\in(0,1)
. We then have
H(W)=|R_{W}|\cdot H(a)
,
\int W=\gamma
, and
\gamma=a|R_{W}|+|O_{W}|
. Let
r^{\ast}:(0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the function defined in
(
1
)
. Since
Lemma

3.4
proves
|R_{W}|\leqslant|O_{W}|
, it follows from
Lemma

3.6
that
H(W)\leqslant r^{\ast}(\gamma)
(specifically, apply
Lemma

3.6
by substituting
c
with
\gamma
,
x
with
|O_{W}|
, and
y
with
|R_{W}|
). Noticing that
H(W)=r^{\ast}(\gamma)
for all
W\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
, the inclusion
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}^{\ast}
follows immediately.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.1
.

By
Lemma

3.7
, it suffices to show that for all
W\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, there is some
W^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
that is equivalent to
W
. Fix
W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}^{\ast}
and
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{16})
. Let
\delta^{\prime}>0
be the constant provided when
Proposition

3.3
is applied with
\epsilon/4
.

First let
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big)
and
\delta:=\min\{\epsilon/4,\delta^{\prime}\}
. We will show that
\delta_{\square}(W,{\mathcal{V}})\leqslant\epsilon
, and this fact together with
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
proves
W
is equivalent to a graphon in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
. By
Lemmas

3.5
and
3.6
,
W
takes the three values
\{0,a,1\}
almost everywhere for some
a\in(0,1)
, and additionally
|R_{W}|=|O_{W}|=\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{10}\gamma
. Hence the equation
\gamma=a|R_{W}|+|O_{W}|
implies
a=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
. Since
t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W)=0
and
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
, there exists a sequence
\{W_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of graphons satisfying the conclusions of
Lemma

2.7
. Let
\eta_{m}
,
\eta^{\prime}_{m}
, and
R_{m}
be the sequences from
Lemma

2.7
. Let
\{W^{\prime}_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a subsequence of
\{W_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
W^{\prime}_{m}\to W
almost everywhere. By Egorov’s theorem, there exists a closed subset
E\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
of measure
|E|>1-\delta/16
such that
W^{\prime}_{m}\to W
uniformly on
E
. Fix
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
sufficiently large such that the following three conditions hold: the number
k
of clusters in the type
R_{m}
associated with
W^{\prime}_{m}
satisfies
k>16/\delta
;
\eta_{m}<\delta/16
; and
|W^{\prime}_{m}-W|<\epsilon/16
on
E
. It follows that

(12)

\lVert W^{\prime}_{m}-W\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{16}\left(1-\frac{\delta}{16}\right)+\frac{\delta}{16}+\frac{1}{k}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{4}\leqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4}\,.

Let
R=([k],E_{R},\sigma)
denote the type associated with
W^{\prime}_{m}
. For all
i,j\in[k]
let

S_{ij}:=\left(\frac{i-1}{k},\frac{i}{k}\right)\times\left(\frac{j-1}{k},\frac{j}{k}\right)

denote the
i,j
block of the unit square. For all
x,y\in[0,1]^{2}
define
i_{xy},\,j_{xy}\in[k]
such that
(x,y)\in S_{i_{xy}j_{xy}}
(we may disregard the set of measure zero where
i_{xy}
and
j_{xy}
are not defined). Define the graphon
g\in{\mathcal{W}}
by

(13)

g(x,y)=\begin{cases}0&S_{i_{xy}j_{xy}}\cap E\neq\emptyset,\,i_{xy}j_{xy}\in E_{R},\,\sigma(i_{xy}j_{xy})=0\\
\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}&S_{i_{xy}j_{xy}}\cap E\neq\emptyset,\,i_{xy}j_{xy}\in E_{R},\,\sigma(i_{xy}j_{xy})=\frac{1}{2}\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Now if
(x,y)
is a point such that
S_{i_{xy}j_{xy}}\cap E\neq\emptyset
, then the fact that
W^{\prime}_{m}
is constant on the squares
S_{ij}
and
|W^{\prime}_{m}-W|<\delta/16
on
E
implies that for some
z\in\{0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\}
, we have
W^{\prime}_{m}\equiv z
identically on
S_{i_{xy}j_{xy}}
. Since at most
\delta k^{2}/16
squares
S_{ij}
do not intersect
E
, and since
R_{m}
has at most
\eta_{m}k^{2}
irregular pairs, we have

(14)

\lVert g-W^{\prime}_{m}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{8}+\eta_{m}+\frac{1}{k}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{4}\leqslant\frac{\epsilon}{4}\,.

Define the red–green–blue coloring
\varphi
of
E(K_{k})
such that edge
ij
is colored red if
g(S_{ij})=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
, green if
g(S_{ij})=0
, and blue if
g(S_{ij})=1
. By the inclusions

R_{W}\subseteq\bBigg@{3}(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}ij\in E_{R}\\
\sigma(ij)=1/2\end{subarray}}S_{ij}\bBigg@{3})\cup\left(\bigcup_{ij\not\in E_{R}}S_{ij}\right)\cup E^{c}\,,\hskip 17.07164pt\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}ij\in E_{R}\\
\sigma(ij)=1\end{subarray}}S_{ij}\subseteq O_{W}\cup\left(\bigcup_{ij\not\in E_{R}}S_{ij}\right)\cup E^{c}\,,

and since
k>16/\epsilon
, we obtain the inequalities

\displaystyle|R_{W}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{e_{r}(\varphi)}{k^{2}}+\frac{\delta}{16}+\frac{1}{k}\leqslant\frac{e_{r}(\varphi)}{k^{2}}+\frac{\delta}{8}\,,

\displaystyle|O_{W}|

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{e_{b}(\varphi)}{k^{2}}-\frac{\delta}{16}-\frac{1}{k}\geqslant\frac{e_{b}(\varphi)}{k^{2}}-\frac{\delta}{8}\,,

where
e_{r}(\varphi)
and
e_{b}(\varphi)
denote the number of red and blue edges of
\varphi
, respectively. Since
|R_{W}|=|O_{W}|
and
\delta k^{2}/32\geqslant k/2
(using our assumption on
k
), it follows that
e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\lfloor k/2\rfloor-\delta k^{2}
. Now
Proposition

3.3
implies
d(\varphi,{\mathcal{F}}(k))\leqslant\epsilon k^{2}/4
, where
{\mathcal{F}}(k)
is the set of edge colorings defined by
(
10
)
. Let
\psi\in{\mathcal{F}}(k)
such that
d(\varphi,\psi)\leqslant\epsilon k^{2}/4
, and let
h\in{\mathcal{W}}
be the graphon defined by

(15)

h(x,y)=\begin{cases}0&(x,y)\in S_{ij},\,\psi(ij)\text{ is green}\\
\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}&(x,y)\in S_{ij},\,\psi(ij)\text{ is red}\\
1&(x,y)\in S_{ij}\,,\psi(ij)\text{ is blue}\end{cases}\,.

It follows that
\lVert g-h\rVert_{1}\leqslant\epsilon/4
. By definition of the set
{\mathcal{F}}(k)
, it is clear that there exists a measure-preserving bijection
\sigma:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that
h\circ(\sigma\otimes\sigma)=W_{\bm{\lambda}}
for some
\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda
(using the definition in
(
7
)
). We thus have
\delta_{\square}(h,{\mathcal{V}})=0
. Combining
(
12
)
,
(
14
)
, and the inequality
\lVert g-h\rVert_{1}\leqslant\epsilon/4
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\square}(W,{\mathcal{V}})

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta_{\square}(W,W^{\prime}_{m})+\delta_{\square}(W^{\prime}_{m},g)+\delta_{\square}(g,h)+\delta_{\square}(h,{\mathcal{V}})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\square}(W,W^{\prime}_{m})+\delta_{\square}(W^{\prime}_{m},g)+\delta_{\square}(g,h)

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert W-W^{\prime}_{m}\rVert_{1}+\lVert W^{\prime}_{m}-g\rVert_{1}+\lVert g-h\rVert_{1}\leqslant\epsilon\,.

Since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary and
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
, we have
\delta_{\square}(W,{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma})=0
, proving that
W
is equivalent to a graphon in
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
.

The proof for the case
\gamma\in\big[\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4},1\big)
is very similar and we describe the parts that are different. Redefine
\delta:=\frac{1}{4}\min\left\{\epsilon,\,\delta^{\prime},\,2\gamma-1\right\}
.
Lemmas

3.5
and
3.6
prove that
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
takes the two values
\{2\gamma-1,1\}
almost everywhere. We again obtain a sequence
\{W_{m}\}_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
satisfying the conclusions of
Lemma

2.7
. Define
W^{\prime}_{m}
,
E
, and
R
in the same way as above. Instead of using
g
as defined in
(
13
)
, we define the graphon
g^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}
by

g^{\prime}(x,y)=\begin{cases}2\gamma-1&S_{i_{xy}j_{xy}}\cap E\neq\emptyset,\,i_{xy}j_{xy}\in E_{R},\,\sigma(i_{xy}j_{xy})=\frac{1}{2}\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Define the edge coloring
\varphi
of
E(K_{k})
in the same way as above, and we again obtain
e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\lfloor k/2\rfloor-\delta k^{2}
. Let
\xi^{\prime}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to[0,1]
be the step function defined by

\xi^{\prime}(x,y)=\begin{cases}1&(x,y)\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)^{2}\cup\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)^{2}\\[3.0pt]
2\gamma-1&(x,y)\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)\times\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\cup\big(\frac{1}{2},1\big)\times\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)\\[3.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

For all
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots)\in\Lambda
, define the graphon
W^{\prime}_{\bm{\lambda}}\in{\mathcal{W}}
by

W^{\prime}_{\bm{\lambda}}(x,y)=\sum_{0\leqslant i<|\bm{\lambda}|}\xi^{\prime}\left(\frac{x-\lambda_{i-1}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i-1}},\frac{y-\lambda_{i-1}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i-1}}\right)\,,

and let
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
denote the set of all such graphons. It follows that the graphon
h
defined in the same way as in
(
15
)
(but with the new coloring
\varphi
) satisfies
\delta_{\square}(h,{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime})=0
. Since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary we have
\delta_{\square}(W,{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime})=0
. Since
W
takes the two values
\{2\gamma-1,1\}
almost everywhere and
W^{\ast}_{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
(defined in
(
8
)
) is the unique graphon in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}
with that same property, it follows that
W
is equivalent to
W^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, proving
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}\subseteq\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}_{\gamma}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

According to
Proposition

3.1
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
is the set of graphons (up to equivalence) achieving the supremum in the variational problem
(
6
)
. It is easy to calculate that for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
and all
W\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
, we have
H(W)=r^{\ast}(\gamma)
. The result now follows immediately from
Proposition

2.11
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.3
.

The conclusions of the theorem are immediate from the proof of
Theorem

1.1
, which shows
\phi(\gamma)=r^{\ast}(\gamma)
, and
Proposition

3.1
, which characterizes the optimal graphons achieving
\phi(\gamma)
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

Define the set of claw-free graphons
{\mathcal{X}}:=\{W\in{\mathcal{W}}:t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W)=0\}
so that the variational problem
\psi(p)
can be stated

(16)

\psi(p)=\sup\{I_{p}(W):W\in{\mathcal{X}}\}\,.

As in the variational problem
(
6
)
, the compactness of
{\mathcal{X}}
and upper semicontinuity of
I_{p}
imply the set of maximizers of
(
16
)
, denoted
{\mathcal{X}}^{p}_{\ast}
, is nonempty. Let
r^{\ast}:(0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the function defined in
(
1
)
. We compute

\displaystyle\psi(p)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\gamma\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]}\,\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}}I_{p}(f)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\gamma\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]}\,\sup_{f\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}}\left\{H(f)+\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)\int_{[0,1]^{2}}f+\log_{2}(1-p)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\gamma\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]}\left\{\sup\{H(f):f\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}\}+\gamma\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)\right\}+\log_{2}(1-p)

(17)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\gamma\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]}\left\{r^{\ast}(\gamma)+\gamma\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)\right\}+\log_{2}(1-p)\,.

Notice that for all
p\in(0,1)
such that

(18)

\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)+\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{10}H\left(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\right)<0\,,

the quantity
r^{\ast}(\gamma)+\gamma\log_{2}\big(\frac{p}{1-p}\big)
is negative for all
\gamma\in[0,1]
and the supremum in
(
17
)
is achieved at
\gamma=0
; in this case
(
17
)
equals
\log_{2}(1-p)
.
Equation

18
holds if and only if
p\in\big(0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\big)
. Equality in
(
18
)
holds if and only if
p=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
, in which case the supremum in
(
17
)
is achieved by all
\gamma\in\big(0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{4}\big]
and again takes the value
\log_{2}(1-p)
. For all
p\in\big(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\big)
, the equation

\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\left(r^{\ast}(\gamma)+\gamma\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)\right)=0

implies
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}(1+p)
, so we deduce that

\psi(p)=r^{\ast}\left(\frac{1+p}{2}\right)+\frac{1+p}{2}\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)+\log_{2}(1-p)=\frac{\log_{2}p}{2}\,.

Hence we have proven
\psi(p)=-r_{\ast}(p)
, where
r_{\ast}
is the function defined in
(
2
)
. By directly applying
Proposition

2.10
, this completes the proof of
Theorem

1.2
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.4
.

The equality
\psi(p)=-r_{\ast}(p)
was justified in the proof of
Theorem

1.2
, and the proof also characterized the set of optimal graphons (up to equivalence):

(19)

{\mathcal{X}}_{\ast}^{p}=\begin{cases}\{W_{0}\}&p\in\big(0,\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\big)\\[5.0pt]
\bigcup_{\gamma\in\big[0,\frac{5-\sqrt{5}}{8}\big]}{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}&p=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\\[5.0pt]
{\mathcal{V}}_{(1+p)/2}&p\in\big(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2},1\big)\end{cases}\,,

where
W_{0}\equiv 0
is the all-zero graphon,
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}:=\{W_{0}\}
, and
{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma}
is as defined after
(
8
)
. This completes the proof of
Theorem

1.4
.
∎

A key consequence of the solutions to the variational problems
\phi(\gamma)
and
\psi(p)
is that the optimizers give a rough structural description of claw-free graphs. The following propositions are adaptations of
[
18
, Theorem 3.1]
. As in the preceding proofs, let
{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
{\mathcal{X}}^{p}_{\ast}
denote the sets of optimal graphons for the variational problems
\phi(\gamma)
and
\psi(p)
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. Let
G
be the uniformly random element of
{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
. For all
\epsilon>0
and large enough
n
,

{\mathbb{P}}\{\delta_{\square}(G,{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma})\geqslant\epsilon\}\leqslant e^{-Cn^{2}}\,,

where
C>0
is a constant depending only on
\epsilon
and
\gamma
.

Proof.

Let
G\sim G(n,1/2)
. Fix
\epsilon>0
and define the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{\epsilon}(n,m):=\{G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m):\delta_{\square}(G,{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma})\geqslant\epsilon\}
. Let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\epsilon}\subseteq{\mathcal{W}}
denote the set of graphons that are limits of sequences of graphs in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\epsilon}(n,m)
. Using
Theorem

1.1
, and applying
Lemma

2.9
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\epsilon}
, we compute that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{\delta_{\square}(G,{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma})\geqslant\epsilon\,|\,G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)\}
