\displaystyle\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}\cdot\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{\epsilon n^{2}}\cdot n^{2}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\epsilon^{7/8}}{8}n^{2}\bigg)\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\epsilon n^{4}\left(\frac{e}{2\epsilon}\right)^{\epsilon n^{2}}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\epsilon^{7/8}}{8}n^{2}\bigg)\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}\leqslant e^{-\beta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|\,,

where we used
(
32
)
and the fact that
\epsilon^{7/8}\gg\epsilon
as
\epsilon\to 0
, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

For all
W\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
,

|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi}|\leqslant e^{-\xi n}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|\,,

where
\xi>0
is a constant, hence

N_{\tau}(n,m,W)\leqslant(1+e^{-\xi n})\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|\,.

Proof.

We first introduce definitions that will be used throughout the proof. Denote
\Pi=\{\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{\ell^{\prime}}\}
,
\Pi_{i}=\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
,
V_{i}:=A_{i}\cup B_{i}
, and
v_{i}:=|V_{i}|
for all
i\in[\ell^{\prime}]
. Let
\ell\in[\ell^{\prime}]
be the greatest index such that
v_{\ell}\geqslant\eta n
. Define the set of graphs

{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}:=\{T(G)\cup G[\Pi_{\mathsf{sp}}]:G\in{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi}\setminus({\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}\cup{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime\prime}_{W,\Pi})\}\,.

For all
T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}
and
i\in[\ell]
, let
M_{i}(T)
denote a canonically chosen maximum matching of the graph
T[V_{i}]
. Let
X_{i}(T):=V(M_{i}(T))
denote the set of vertices covered by
M_{i}(T)
.

For all
v\in V
, define the set

{\mathsf{High}}(v):=\{i\in[\ell]:\text{$d_{T}(v,A_{i})>(1-\alpha)|A_{i}|$ or $d_{T}(v,B_{i})>(1-\alpha)|B_{i}|$}\}\,.

Notice that for all
G\in C_{W,\Pi}\,
, if
v\in V_{i}
and
j\in{\mathsf{High}}(v)
then the optimality of
\Pi=\Pi(G)
implies
v_{i}\geqslant v_{j}/4
(since otherwise one could obtain a more optimal division for
G
by moving
v
to
V_{j}
). Let
r\in[\ell^{\prime}]
be the greatest index such that
v_{r}\geqslant\eta n/4
. By definition of
r
, the set
{\mathsf{High}}(v)
is empty whenever
i>r
and
v\in V_{i}
.

For all
T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}
and
i\in[r]
, define the set

V_{i}^{\mathsf{hi}}(T):=\left\{v\in V_{i}:{\mathsf{High}}(v)\neq\emptyset\right\}\,,

and let
D_{i}(T)\subseteq V_{i}^{\mathsf{hi}}(T)
be a subset of either
A_{i}
or
B_{i}
of size exactly
|V_{i}^{\mathsf{hi}}(T)|/2
. For all
T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}
, let
T_{\mathsf{hi}}
be the graph on the edge set

E(T_{\mathsf{hi}})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}\,\bigcup_{v\in V_{i}^{\mathsf{hi}}(T)}\,\bigcup_{j\in{\mathsf{High}}(v)}E_{T}(v,V_{j})\,,

where the vertex set of
T_{\mathsf{hi}}
is induced by its edges. Also let
T_{\mathsf{lo}}
be the graph on the edge set
E(T)\setminus E(T_{\mathsf{hi}})
, where the vertex set is again induced by the edge set.

For all
i\in[r]
,
v\in D_{i}(T)
(we may assume
v\in A_{i}
), and
j\in{\mathsf{High}}(v)
, there exists a vertex
h_{v}\in N_{T}(v,V_{j})
such that
d_{T}(h_{v},B_{i})\leqslant\alpha|B_{i}|
; indeed, if there were no such vertex
h_{v}
then we would have

e(T)\geqslant e_{T}(N_{T}(v,V_{j}),B_{i})\geqslant|N_{T}(v,V_{j})|\cdot\alpha|B_{i}|\geqslant\frac{\eta n}{4}\cdot\frac{\alpha\eta n}{16}>\epsilon n^{2}\,,

which contradicts the fact that
e(T)\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}
.

Define the vertex set

S:=\Pi_{\mathsf{sp}}\cup\bigcup_{\ell<i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}}V_{i}\,.

For all vectors
\bm{d}\in\{0,1,2\dots\}^{\binom{\ell}{2}+2\ell+r}
we will index
\bm{d}
using the components
d_{ij}
for
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant\ell
, the components
d_{i}
and
d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}
for
i\in[\ell]
, and the components
d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}
for
i\in[r]
.
For all such
\bm{d}
, define the set of graphs

{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}:=\left\{T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}:\begin{multlined}\text{for all $i\in[\ell]$, $j\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}$, and $k\in[r]$, $e_{T_{\mathsf{lo}}}(V_{i},V_{j})=d_{ij}$}\vphantom{\displaystyle\bigcup}\\
\\
\text{$|X_{i}(T)|=d_{i}$, $|V_{k}^{\mathsf{hi}}(T)|=d_{{\mathsf{hi}},k}$, and $e_{T_{\mathsf{lo}}}(V_{i},S)=d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}$}\end{multlined}\right\}\,.

Fix
T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}
and define the quantity

t:=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant\ell^{\prime}}e(T[V_{i},V_{j}])+e(T[V,\Pi_{\mathsf{sp}}])-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime}}e(T[V_{i}])\,.

For all
\bm{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}
let
G_{\Pi,T,\bm{m}}
be the random graph from
Definition

6.3
.
Lemma

6.2
proves that every graph
G\in{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,T}
is of the form
(
33
)
for some
\bm{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}
and
H\subseteq\Pi
, hence

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,T}|\leqslant\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}}{\mathbb{P}}\{G_{\Pi,T,\bm{m}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(n)\}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}\,.

For all
T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}
and
i\in[r]
, let
J_{i}:=\{h_{v}:v\in D_{i}(T)\}
and assume without loss of generality
D_{i}(T)\subseteq A_{i}
. We can then apply
Lemma

4.3
with the following variable assignments:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\Pi\leftarrow\Pi_{i}&m\leftarrow m_{i}&D\leftarrow D_{i}(T)&D^{\prime}\leftarrow J_{i}&F^{\prime}\leftarrow T[J_{i},B_{i}]\\[10.0pt]
k\leftarrow d_{i}&d\leftarrow d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}/2&l\leftarrow d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}&C\leftarrow V\setminus V_{i}&F\leftarrow T_{\mathsf{lo}}[V_{i},V\setminus V_{i}]\,.\end{array}

Similarly for
\ell<i\leqslant r
, we apply
Lemma

4.3
with the variable assignments

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\Pi\leftarrow\Pi_{i}&m\leftarrow m_{i}&D\leftarrow D_{i}(T)&D^{\prime}\leftarrow J_{i}&F^{\prime}\leftarrow T[J_{i},B_{i}]\\[10.0pt]
k\leftarrow 0&d\leftarrow d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}/2&l\leftarrow 0&C\leftarrow\emptyset&F\leftarrow\emptyset\,.\end{array}

For all
i\in[\ell]
define
q_{i}:=\eta^{2}\big(\sum_{j\neq i}d_{ij}+d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}\big)
. Since the random bipartite graphs
H_{1},\dots,H_{r}
from the definition of
G_{\Pi,T,\bm{m}}
are all independent, the aforementioned applications of
Lemma

4.3
imply

(34)

{\mathbb{P}}\{G_{\Pi,T,\bm{m}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(n)\}\leqslant\left(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{d_{i}v_{i}/2^{11}}\exp\left(-\frac{p_{i}^{4}}{2^{21}}\cdot v_{i}\cdot\min\left\{q_{i}\,,\,v_{i}\right\}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}p^{d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}v_{i}/64}\right)\,,

where
p_{i}:=m_{i}/(|A_{i}|\cdot|B_{i}|)
for all
i\in[r]
.

For all
i\in[\ell]
,
j\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}
, and
k\in[r]
, define the sets of graphs

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{i}&:=\{T[V_{i}]:T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}\}\,,\\[10.0pt]
{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{{\mathsf{sp}},i}&:=\{T_{\mathsf{lo}}[V_{i},\,S]:T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}\}\,,\\[10.0pt]
{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{ij}&:=\{T_{\mathsf{lo}}[V_{i},\,V_{j}]:T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}\}\,,\\[10.0pt]
{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{{\mathsf{hi}},k}&:=\{T_{\mathsf{hi}}[V_{k},\,V\setminus V_{k}]:T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}\}\,,\\[10.0pt]
{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi}^{\mathsf{sp}}&:=\{T[S]:T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}\}\,.\end{array}

We now bound the sizes of these sets. By combining
Lemma

6.2

4
and the definition of
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}
, we know that for all
T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}
,
G\in{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,T}
, and
i\in[\ell]
, every vertex
v\in A_{i}
satisfies
d_{T}(v,A_{i})<\alpha|A_{i}|
. Additionally, since
M_{i}(T)
is a maximum matching of the graph
T[V_{i}]
, every edge
e\in E_{T}(V_{i})
has an endpoint in
X_{i}(T)
. These facts directly imply

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{i}|&\leqslant\sum_{X\in\binom{V_{i}}{d_{i}}}\,\prod_{P\in\Pi_{i}}\,\prod_{v\in P\cap X}\binom{|P|}{\leqslant\alpha|P|}\leqslant\sum_{X\in\binom{V_{i}}{d_{i}}}\,\prod_{P\in\Pi_{i}}\,\prod_{v\in P\cap X}\alpha v_{i}\binom{v_{i}}{\alpha v_{i}}\\
&\leqslant\binom{v_{i}}{d_{i}}\left(\alpha v_{i}\binom{v_{i}}{\alpha v_{i}}\right)^{d_{i}}\leqslant 2^{H(\alpha)d_{i}v_{i}+d_{i}\log_{2}v_{i}+d_{i}\log_{2}\alpha}\leqslant 2^{2H(\alpha)d_{i}v_{i}}\,.\end{split}

We also have the trivial bounds

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{{\mathsf{sp}},i}|&\leqslant\binom{\binom{n}{2}}{d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}}\leqslant\left(\frac{en^{2}}{2d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}}\right)^{d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}}\,,\\
|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{ij}|&\leqslant\binom{v_{i}v_{j}}{d_{ij}}\leqslant\left(\frac{ev_{i}v_{j}}{d_{ij}}\right)^{d_{ij}}\,,\\
|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{{\mathsf{hi}},i}|&\leqslant\binom{n}{(1-\alpha)n}^{d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}}\binom{n}{\alpha n}^{d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}}\leqslant 2^{H(\alpha)\cdot d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}n}\,,\end{split}

which we combine to deduce

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{i}|\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{{\mathsf{sp}},i}|\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant\ell}|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{ij}|\Bigg)\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{r}|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}^{{\mathsf{hi}},i}|\Bigg)\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi}^{\mathsf{sp}}|

(35)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\leqslant\exp\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(2(\log 2)H(\alpha)d_{i}v_{i}+d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}\log\left(\frac{en^{2}}{2d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}}\right)\right)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant\ell}d_{ij}\log\left(\frac{ev_{i}v_{j}}{d_{ij}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}H(\alpha)(\log 2)d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}n\Bigg)\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi}^{\mathsf{sp}}|\,.\end{split}

For all
T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi}
and
\bm{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}
let
G_{\Pi,T,\bm{m}}
be the random graph from
Definition

6.3
. Define the set of graphs

{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}:=\bigcup_{T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,T}\,.

Now by comparing terms pairwise between expressions
(
34
)
and
(
35
)
, we obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{\epsilon n^{2}}\,\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}\\
e(T)=t\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{P}}\{G_{\Pi,T,\bm{m}}\in{\mathcal{C}}(n)\}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p_{i}^{d_{i}v_{i}/2^{11}}\exp\left(-\frac{p_{i}^{4}}{2^{21}}\cdot v_{i}\cdot\min\left\{q_{i}\,,\,v_{i}\right\}\right)\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}p^{d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}v_{i}/64}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\cdot\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-\zeta^{\prime}n}\cdot|{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi}^{\mathsf{sp}}|\cdot\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}\leqslant e^{-\zeta n}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|

for constants
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}>0
. In the above calculation we used the fact that
\eta^{2}d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i}
and
\eta^{2}d_{ij}
each appear in the penalty term involving
q_{i}
; namely, if
v_{i}\leqslant q_{i}
then since
v_{i}\geqslant\eta n
, the penalty from this term is of order
\exp(-\Omega(\alpha n^{2}))
; otherwise if
q_{i}<v_{i}
then the penalty is of order
\exp(-\omega(\eta^{3}(d_{ij}+d_{{\mathsf{sp}},i})n))
; in both cases, the terms from
|{\mathcal{T}}^{{\mathsf{sp}},i}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|
and
|{\mathcal{T}}^{ij}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|
are compensated for. We also used that
H(\alpha)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, so that for small enough
\epsilon>0
,

2(\log 2)H(\alpha)d_{i}v_{i}<\log\left(\frac{1}{p}\right)d_{i}v_{i}\,,

and a similar comparison applies to the terms involving
d_{{\mathsf{hi}},i}
using the fact that
v_{i}\geqslant\eta n
and
\eta\gg H(\alpha)
as
\epsilon\to 0
. The last inequality bounding
|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|
used
(
32
)
(note we used a slightly stronger inequality than
(
32
)
, which holds by definition of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{W,\Pi}^{\mathsf{sp}}
).

Let
\beta>0
be the minimum of the constants from
Lemmas

6.4
and
6.5
. Using the above bound on
|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|
we compute that

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\bm{d}}|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}|+|{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{W,\Pi}|+|{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime\prime}_{W,\Pi}|\leqslant(n^{8r^{2}}e^{-\zeta n}+2e^{-\beta n^{2}})|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|\leqslant e^{-\xi n}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|

for a constant
\xi>0
, where we used the fact that
{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi,\bm{d}}
is nonempty for at most
n^{8r^{2}}
of the vectors
\bm{d}\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}^{\binom{\ell}{2}+2\ell+r}
, completing the proof of the first statement of the lemma.

The second assertion of the lemma follows immediately by observing

|N_{\tau}(n,m,W)|\leqslant\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}}(|{\mathcal{C}}_{W,\Pi}|+|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|)\,,

which uses the statement in
Lemma

6.2
that
\Pi(G)\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
for all
G\in N_{\tau}(n,m,W)
.
∎

The next lemma is one of the key steps of this section and the proof of
Theorem

1.5

3
. It asserts that for any optimal graphon
W\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
that is not equivalent to
W^{\ast}
, if we make
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small then there are at least
n^{\Theta(n)}
more claw-free graphs
G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)
that are close to
W^{\ast}
in cut metric than that are close to
W
.

Lemma 6.7
.

If
W\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
\delta_{\square}(W,W^{\ast})\geqslant 8\omega
then

|N_{\tau}(n,m,W)|\leqslant n^{-\nu n}|N_{\omega}(n,m,W^{\ast})|

for a constant
\nu>0
depending only on
\epsilon
.

Before beginning the proof of
Lemma

6.7
we introduce some more notation and prove one more lemma. Let
W\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
,
\Pi=\{\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{\ell}\}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
,
\Pi_{i}=\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
, and
V_{i}:=A_{i}\cup B_{i}
. Let
\ell^{\prime}\in[\ell]
be the greatest index such that
|V_{\ell^{\prime}}|\geqslant\eta n
(note this is well-defined by definition of
\eta
), and let
\Pi^{\prime}:=\{\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{\ell^{\prime}}\}
. For all such
\Pi
and for all
0\leqslant t\leqslant\binom{n}{2}
, define the set

{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}:=\left\{F\subseteq\Pi^{\prime}:e_{F}(V(\Pi_{i}))=m_{i}\,,\,i\in[\ell^{\prime}]\,,\,\bm{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}\right\}\,,

where
F\subseteq\Pi^{\prime}
means
F
is a subgraph of the disjoint union
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime}}K_{\Pi_{i}}
of bipartite graphs. Define the vertex set

S_{\Pi}:=\Pi_{\mathsf{sp}}\cup\bigcup_{\ell^{\prime}<i\leqslant\ell}V_{i}

and the integer
s(\Pi):=|S_{\Pi}|
. For all
0\leqslant t\leqslant\binom{n}{2}
, define the set of claw-free graphs

{\mathcal{D}}_{\Pi,t}:=\left\{\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime}}G_{i}\right)\sqcup S:G_{i}^{c}\subseteq\Pi_{i}\,,\,e_{G_{i}}(A_{i},B_{i})=m_{i}\,,\bm{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}\,,\,S\in{\mathcal{C}}(s(\Pi),t)\right\}\,,

where
\sqcup
denotes the vertex-disjoint union.

We claim that for all
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{\ast}}
and all
0\leqslant t\leqslant 4\eta n^{2}
,

(36)

|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\cdot|{\mathcal{C}}(s(\Pi),t)|=|{\mathcal{D}}_{\Pi,t}|\leqslant 2\cdot|N_{\omega}(n,m,W^{\ast})|\,.

The equality in
(
36
)
holds automatically by definition of
{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}
,
{\mathcal{D}}_{\Pi,t}
, and
s(\Pi)
. To see the inequality, first note that by
Lemma

6.2

1
every
\Pi=\{\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{\ell}\}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{\ast}}
satisfies
||V(\Pi_{1})|-2\mu n|\leqslant\delta n
and
|V(\Pi_{i})|\leqslant 8\delta n
for all
i\geqslant 2
. Also, a uniformly random element
G
of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\Pi,t}
has regularly distributed edges
E(G[\Pi_{i}])
for all
i\in[\ell]
with high probability. It follows that
\widehat{\delta}_{\square}(G,W^{\ast}_{n})\leqslant\omega/2
with high probability, and since
\delta_{\square}(G_{1},G_{2})\leqslant\widehat{\delta}_{\square}(G_{1},G_{2})
always, inequality
(
36
)
follows.

For positive numbers
X(n,\epsilon)
and
Y(n,\epsilon)
, define the relation
X(n,\epsilon)\preceq Y(n,\epsilon)
to mean

(37)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log Y(n,\epsilon)-\log X(n,\epsilon)}{n\log n}\geqslant 0\,.

For all
U\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, let
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{U}
denote the set of all
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{U}
such that
\min\{|V(\Pi^{\prime})|:\Pi^{\prime}\in\Pi\}\geqslant\epsilon n
. Notice the cardinality of
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{U}
is small with respect to the relation
\preceq
in the following sense: if we let
N:=\lceil 1/\epsilon\rceil+1
then

(38)

\displaystyle|{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{U}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}+\cdots+x_{N}=n\\
x_{i}\geqslant 0\end{subarray}}\binom{n}{x_{1},\dots,x_{N}}2^{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{N}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(n+N)^{N}\cdot 2^{H(x_{1}/n\,,\dots,\,x_{N}/n)n}\cdot 2^{Nn}\preceq 1\,,

where we used a standard bound on the multinomial coefficient (see e.g.
[
23
, Lemma 2.2]
) and
H(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=-\sum_{i}y_{i}\log_{2}y_{i}
is the entropy.

Lemma 6.8
.

For all
W,W^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and all
0\leqslant t\leqslant 4\eta n^{2}
,

(39)

\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\succeq\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W^{\prime}}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi^{\prime},t}|\,,

where the relation
\succeq
is meant in the sense of
(
37
)
.

Proof.

Let
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots)\in\Lambda
be the unique sequence such that
W
is equivalent to
W_{\bm{\lambda}}
(using the definition of
W_{\bm{\lambda}}
in
Equation

7
). Denote
\Pi=\{\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{q}\}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
and let
\ell\in[q]
be the greatest index such that
|V(\Pi_{\ell})|\geqslant\eta n
. Denote
\Pi^{\prime}=\{\Pi^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\Pi^{\prime}_{r}\}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{\prime}}
and let
\ell^{\prime}\in[r]
be the greatest index such that
|V(\Pi_{\ell^{\prime}})|\geqslant\eta n
. By definition of
{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}
we have

|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}\hskip 19.91692pt\text{and}\hskip 19.91692pt|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi^{\prime},t}|=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi^{\prime},t}}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime}}\binom{e(\Pi^{\prime}_{i})}{m_{i}}\,.

Using
(
38
)
and
|{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi^{\prime},t}|\leqslant(2\delta n)^{\ell}
, it follows that
(
39
)
is implied by the assertion

(40)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n\log_{2}n}\left(\min\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\log_{2}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}-\max\sum_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime}}\log_{2}\binom{e(\Pi^{\prime}_{i})}{m^{\prime}_{i}}\right)\geqslant 0\,,

where the minimum is over all
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W}
and
\bm{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}
, and the maximum is over all
\Pi^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W^{\prime}}
and
\bm{m}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Pi,t}
. Notice that

(41)

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\log_{2}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left(H\left(\frac{m_{i}}{e(\Pi_{i})}\right)e(\Pi_{i})+O(n)\right)\,,

which follows easily from Stirling’s formula. As
\epsilon\to 0
and
n\to\infty
, we have
m_{i}/e(\Pi_{i})\to\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
and
e(\Pi_{i})\sim(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j-1})^{2}n^{2}/4
for some
j\geqslant 1
by
Lemma

6.2

1
, which implies
(
41
)
is asymptotic to
H(W)\binom{n}{2}+O(n)
. Inequality
(
40
)
now follows since
H(W)=H(W^{\prime})
.
∎

The main result of
[
50
]
is an asymptotic formula for the number
\kappa(n)
of cubic claw-free graphs on
2n
vertices:

\kappa(n)\sim\frac{(2n)!}{e\sqrt{6\pi n}}\left(\frac{n}{2e}\right)^{n/3}e^{(n/2)^{1/3}}=e^{\frac{7}{3}n\log n+O(n)}\,.

The asymptotics of
\kappa(n)
play a key role in the proof of
Lemma

6.7
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.7
.

Let
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots)\in\Lambda
be the unique sequence such that
W
is equivalent to
W_{\bm{\lambda}}
(using the definition of
W_{\bm{\lambda}}
in
Equation

7
). Let
1\leqslant j\leqslant 1/\eta
be the greatest index such that
\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j-1}\geqslant\eta
(this is well-defined by definition of
\eta
), and let
\lambda:=\lambda_{j}
. We claim that
\lambda\geqslant 2\mu+\omega
. Suppose to the contrary
\lambda<2\mu+\omega
. Then we must have
\lambda_{1}\geqslant 2\mu-\omega
, since otherwise

\displaystyle t(K_{2},W)

\displaystyle\leqslant(2\mu-\omega)^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\bigg(1+\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\bigg)+4\omega^{2}+4\eta=\gamma+\frac{37-\sqrt{5}}{4}\cdot\eta-(5-\sqrt{5})\mu\omega<\gamma\,.

But the inequalities
2\mu-\omega\leqslant\lambda_{1}\leqslant\lambda<2\mu+\omega
imply
\delta_{\square}(W,W^{\ast})\leqslant\lVert W-W^{\ast}\rVert_{1}<8\omega
, a contradiction. We thus have
\lambda\geqslant 2\mu+\omega
.

Let
k:=\lfloor\omega n/4\rfloor
and
s:=\lceil(1-2\mu-\omega+\alpha)n\rceil
. It is straightforward from
Lemma

6.2

1
and the inequality
\lambda\geqslant 2\mu+\omega
that
s(\Pi)\leqslant s
for all
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
. Note also that every division
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
satisfies
V(\Pi)\geqslant(2\mu-\delta)n+2k
, and every division
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{\ast}}
satisfies
s(\Pi)\geqslant s+2k
. By definition of
S_{\Pi}
, every graph
G\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}
satisfies
e_{G}(S_{\Pi})\leqslant 4\eta n^{2}
, which directly implies

|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|\leqslant\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\cdot|{\mathcal{C}}(s(\Pi),t)|\leqslant\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\cdot|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|\,.

We will use that for all
U\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{U}
,
k^{2k}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\leqslant|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t+3k}|\cdot\kappa(k)
, which follows from
Lemma

A.1
since for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell^{\prime}
,

k^{2k}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}}\binom{e(\Pi_{i})}{m_{i}-3k}^{-1}\leqslant k^{2k}\exp\left(-\left(1-\frac{e(\Pi_{i})-m_{i}+3k}{m_{i}-3k}\right)3k\right)=k^{2k}e^{O(n)}\leqslant\kappa(k)\,.

Let
N_{1}(n):=N_{\omega}(n,m,W^{\ast})
and
N_{2}(n):=N_{\tau}(n,m,W)
. Using
Lemma

6.6
,
Lemma

6.8
,
(
36
)
, and
(
38
)
, and using the relation
\preceq
in the sense of
(
37
)
, we compute

\displaystyle k^{2k}\cdot|N_{2}(n)|

\displaystyle\preceq k^{2k}\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}|

\displaystyle\leqslant k^{2k}\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W}}\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\cdot|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|

\displaystyle\preceq k^{2k}\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant 4\eta n^{2}}\left\{|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\right\}

\displaystyle\preceq k^{2k}\cdot\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W^{\ast}}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|

\displaystyle\preceq\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W^{\ast}}}\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}-3k}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t+3k}|\cdot|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|\cdot\kappa(k)

\displaystyle\preceq\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W^{\ast}}}\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\cdot|{\mathcal{C}}(s(\Pi),t)|\preceq|N_{1}(n)|\,.

Since
k=\lfloor\omega n/4\rfloor
, it directly follows that
|N_{2}(n)|\leqslant n^{-\nu n}|N_{1}(n)|
for a constant
\nu>0
depending only on
\epsilon
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.5

3
.

Let
{\mathcal{N}}=\{W^{\ast},W_{1},\dots,W_{k}\}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
be a
\tau
-net of
{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
in cut metric. Define the sets

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{close}}&:=\displaystyle N_{\tau}(n,m,W^{\ast})\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}N_{\tau}(n,m,W_{i})\,,\\[15.0pt]
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{far}}&:={\mathcal{C}}(n,m)\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{close}}\,.\end{array}

Let
I:=\{i\in[k]:\delta_{\square}(W_{i},W^{\ast})\geqslant 8\omega\}
and
\tau^{\prime}:=\tau+8\omega
, so that

|{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{close}}|\leqslant|N_{\tau^{\prime}}(n,m,W^{\ast})|+\sum_{i\in I}|N_{\tau}(n,m,W_{i})|\,.

For
\epsilon>0
small enough, the parameter
\tau^{\prime}
is small enough that
Lemma

6.6
implies

|N_{\tau^{\prime}}(n,m,W^{\ast})|\leqslant(1+e^{-\beta n})\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{\ast}}}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W^{\ast},\Pi}|\,,

where the sets
{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W^{\ast},\Pi}
are understood to be redefined with
\tau^{\prime}
.
Let
C>0
be the constant from
Proposition

3.8
and let
\nu>0
be the minimum of the constants when
Lemma

6.7
is applied to the graphons
W_{i}
,
i\in I
. We compute that

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{C}}(n,m)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{close}}|+|{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{far}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant|N_{\tau^{\prime}}(n,m,W^{\ast})|+\sum_{i\in I}|N_{\tau}(n,m,W_{i})|+|{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathsf{far}}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big(1+kn^{-\nu n}+e^{-Cn^{2}}\big)|N_{\tau^{\prime}}(n,m,W^{\ast})|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big(1+kn^{-\nu n}+e^{-Cn^{2}}\big)(1+e^{-\beta n})\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{\ast}}}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W^{\ast},\Pi}|\,.

From
Lemma

6.2

1
we know that every
\Pi=\{\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{\ell}\}\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{\ast}}
satisfies
||V(\Pi_{1})|-2\mu n|\leqslant\delta n
and
|V(\Pi_{i})|\leqslant 8\delta n
for all
i\geqslant 2
, proving the first statement of
Theorem

1.5

3
. The statement that almost every
G\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W^{\ast},\Pi}
has
e_{G}(S_{\Pi})=\Omega(n)
follows from the argument already carried out in the proof of
Lemma

6.7
, namely the use of cubic claw-free graphs to establish a lower-bound on
|N_{\omega}(n,m,W^{\ast})|
.
∎

7.
The Conditional Erdős–Rényi Random Graph

In this section we prove
Theorem

1.6
. Several definitions and results from
Section

6
are used throughout. Define the parameter
\rho:=\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}
. If
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq{\mathcal{G}}(n)
is a set of graphs on
n
vertices and
p\in(0,1)
then define the quantity

Z_{p}({\mathcal{F}}):=(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{F}}}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{e(G)}\,.

For all
\tau>0
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and graphons
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
, define the neighborhood

N_{\tau}(n,W):=\{G\in{\mathcal{C}}(n):\delta_{\square}(G,W)<\tau\}\,.

Proof of
Theorem

1.6
.

Throughout the proof, let
p\in(0,1)
and let
G
be the Erdős–Rényi random graph
G(n,p)
conditioned on being claw-free.

In the range
p\in(0,\rho)
,
Proposition

3.9
proves
\delta_{\square}(G,W_{0})<\epsilon
with probability at least
e^{-Cn^{2}}
for a constant
C=C(\epsilon,p)>0
, which proves
G
has less than
\epsilon n^{2}
edges with high probability.

Next assume
p\in\big(\rho,1\big)
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,(p-\rho)/2^{64})
and
\tau:=\frac{1}{16}(\epsilon/32)^{67}
. Let
Z_{p}:=Z_{p}({\mathcal{C}}(n))
,
\gamma:=(1+p)/2
, and let
U_{p}:=W^{\ast}_{\gamma}
(where
W^{\ast}_{\gamma}
is the function defined in
(
8
)
), so that
U_{p}
is the unique optimal graphon (up to equivalence) in the variational problem
(
16
)
. Note also that
U_{p}
has edge density
\gamma
. Define the quantities

\begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{\mathsf{close}}&:=Z_{p}(N_{\tau}(n,U_{p}))\,,\\[8.0pt]
Z_{\mathsf{far}}&:=Z_{p}({\mathcal{C}}(n)\setminus N_{\tau}(n,U_{p}))\,.\end{array}

By the counting lemma for graphons
[
41
, Lemma 10.23]
, we know that for all
G\in N_{\tau}(n,U_{p})
,
\big|e(G)-\gamma\binom{n}{2}\big|\leqslant\tau
. Also, letting
m:=e(G)
, we have
G\in N_{8\tau}(n,m,W_{2m/n^{2}})
. From the proof of
Theorem

1.5

1
, we see that

|N_{8\tau}(n,m,W_{2m/n^{2}})|\leqslant(1+e^{-\xi n})|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|

for a constant
\xi>0
. If
C=C(\epsilon,p)>0
is the constant from
Proposition

3.9
then

\displaystyle Z_{p}

\displaystyle=Z_{\mathsf{close}}+Z_{\mathsf{far}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}|N_{8\tau}(n,m,W_{2m/n^{2}})|\cdot\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m}+e^{-Cn^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+e^{-\xi n})(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|\cdot\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m}+e^{-Cn^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+e^{-\xi n})\cdot Z_{p}({\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n))+e^{-Cn^{2}}\,,

which proves
Z_{p}=(1+o(1))\cdot Z_{p}({\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n))
, hence
G
is co-bipartite with high probability.

Finally we examine the case
p=\rho
. Let
W_{0}
denote the all-zero graphon and let
W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\ast}^{p}
be a graphon such that
\delta_{\square}(W,W_{0})\geqslant 8\omega
, which implies
W
has edge density at least
8\omega
. We may assume
W
is of the form
W_{\bm{\lambda}}
for some
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\dots)\in\Lambda
as defined in
(
7
)
. Let
\gamma:=\int W
be the edge density of
W
. Notice that
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\omega
since if the opposite were to hold, then
\lambda_{i+1}-\lambda_{i}<\omega
for all
0\leqslant i<|\bm{\lambda}|
, which would imply
t(K_{2},W)\leqslant 4\omega
, a contradiction.

Below, we write the sets
{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W,\Pi}
and
{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}
as they were defined in
Section

6
, i.e. with respect to the number of edges
m
, since these sets appear in a summation over
m
.

For all
(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}\leqslant m\leqslant(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}
, let
W^{m}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{2m/n^{2}}
denote an optimal graphon with edge density
2m/n^{2}
such that
\delta_{\square}(W^{m},W)\leqslant 6\tau
. If
G\in N_{\tau}(n,W)
then
\big|e(G)-\gamma\binom{n}{2}|\leqslant{\tau}
and
G\in N_{8\tau}(n,m,W^{m})
, hence by
Lemma

6.6
we have

\displaystyle|N_{\tau}(n,W)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}|N_{8\tau}(n,m,W^{m})|\leqslant(1+e^{-\xi n})\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}\,\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{m}}}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W^{m},\Pi}|\,.

Let
k:=\lfloor\omega n/4\rfloor
and
s:=\lceil(1-\omega+\alpha)n\rceil
. It is straightforward from
Lemma

6.2

1
and the inequality
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\omega
that
s(\Pi)\leqslant s
for all
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
, and that
s+2k\leqslant n
. Note that every division
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W}
satisfies
V(\Pi)\geqslant 2k
. By definition of
S_{\Pi}
, every graph
G\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W^{m},\Pi}
satisfies
e_{G}(s(\Pi))\leqslant 4\eta n^{2}
. Let
N_{1}:=N_{\omega}(n,W_{0})
and
N_{2}:=N_{\tau}(n,W)
. For all
U\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, let
{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{U}
be the set of all
\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{U}
such that
\min\{|V(\Pi^{\prime})|:\Pi^{\prime}\in\Pi\}\geqslant\epsilon n
. By an argument very similar to
Lemma

6.8
, and using the fact that
I_{p}(W_{0})=I_{p}(W)=I_{p}(W^{m})=0
, we have that for all
0\leqslant t\leqslant 4\eta n^{2}
,

(42)

1\preceq\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}\,\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m}\preceq\sum_{m=0}^{\eta n^{2}}\,\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W_{0}}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m}\preceq 1\,,

where the relation
\succeq
is meant in the sense of
(
37
)
.

Using
(
38
)
and
(
42
)
, and using the relation
\preceq
in the sense of
(
37
)
, we compute

\displaystyle k^{2k}\cdot Z(N_{2})

\displaystyle\preceq(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}\,\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}_{W^{m}}}|{\mathcal{C}}^{\ast}_{W^{m},\Pi}|\cdot k^{2k}\cdot\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\preceq(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}\,\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W^{m}}}\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\cdot|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|\cdot k^{2k}\cdot\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\preceq(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\max_{0\leqslant t\leqslant 4\eta n^{2}}\left\{|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|\cdot k^{2k}\sum_{m=(\gamma-{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+{\tau})\binom{n}{2}}\,\sum_{\Pi\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\prime}_{W^{m}}}|{\mathcal{R}}_{\Pi,t}|\cdot\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m}\right\}

\displaystyle\preceq(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{C}}(s,t)|\cdot\kappa(k)\preceq(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{4\eta n^{2}}|{\mathcal{C}}(n,t)|\preceq Z(N_{1})\,.

Since
k=\lfloor\omega n/4\rfloor
, it follows that
Z(N_{2})\leqslant n^{-\zeta n}\cdot Z(N_{1})
for a constant
\zeta>0
.

To complete the proof in the case
p=\rho
, let
{\mathcal{N}}=\{W_{0},W_{1},\dots,W_{k}\}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}^{p}_{\ast}
be a
\tau
-net of
{\mathcal{X}}^{p}_{\ast}
in cut metric. Define the quantities

\begin{array}[]{ll}Z^{\prime}_{\mathsf{close}}&:=\displaystyle Z_{p}\left(N_{\tau}(n,W_{0})\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}N_{\tau}(n,W_{i})\right)\\[18.0pt]
Z^{\prime}_{\mathsf{far}}&:=Z_{p}({\mathcal{C}}(n))-Z^{\prime}_{\mathsf{close}}\,.\end{array}

Let
I:=\{i\in[k]:\delta_{\square}(W_{i}\,,\,W^{\ast})\geqslant 8\omega\}
and
\tau^{\prime}:=\tau+8\omega
, so that

Z^{\prime}_{\mathsf{close}}\leqslant Z_{p}(N_{\tau^{\prime}}(n,W_{0}))+\sum_{i\in I}Z_{p}(N_{\tau}(n,W_{i}))\,.

If
C>0
is the constant from
Proposition

3.9
then

\displaystyle Z_{p}({\mathcal{C}}(n))

\displaystyle\leqslant Z^{\prime}_{\mathsf{close}}+Z^{\prime}_{\mathsf{far}}

\displaystyle\leqslant Z_{p}(N_{\tau^{\prime}}(n,W_{0}))+\sum_{i\in I}Z_{p}(N_{\tau}(n,W_{i}))+Z^{\prime}_{\mathsf{far}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\big(1+kn^{-\zeta n}+e^{-Cn^{2}}\big)\cdot Z_{p}(N_{\tau^{\prime}}(n,W_{0}))\,,

which directly implies
\delta_{\square}(G,W_{0})<\epsilon
with high probability, completing the proof.
∎

8.
Extremal Structure and Stability of Edge Colorings

Let
\varphi
be a red–green–blue coloring of
E(K_{n})
. For all
u\in V(K_{n})
and
U\subseteq V(K_{n})
define

\displaystyle N_{r}(u)

\displaystyle:=\{v\in V(K_{n}):\varphi(uv)\text{ is red}\}\,,

\displaystyle d_{r}(u)

\displaystyle:=|N_{r}(u)|\,,

\displaystyle e_{r}(U)

\displaystyle:=|\{e\in\textstyle\binom{U}{2}:\varphi(e)\text{ is red}\}|\,.

We will also use the notation
N_{r}(u,U):=N_{r}(u)\cap U
and
d_{r}(u,U):=|N_{r}(u,U)|
. Define the green and blue counterparts
N_{g}(u)
,
N_{b}(u)
, etc. analogously. Let
E_{r}(\varphi)
,
E_{g}(\varphi)
, and
E_{b}(\varphi)
denote the sets of red, green, and blue edges, respectively, of the coloring
\varphi
, and let
e_{r}(\varphi)
,
e_{g}(\varphi)
, and
e_{b}(\varphi)
denote the respective cardinalities of those sets. We now prove
Lemma

3.2
, which generalizes Mantel’s theorem to red–green–blue edge colorings: Mantel’s theorem is obtained by considering only those
\varphi
with
e_{g}(\varphi)=0
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.2
.

Define the sets
L_{1}:=\{u:d_{r}(u)<d_{b}(u)+1\}
,
L_{2}:=\{u:d_{r}(u)=d_{b}(u)+1\}
, and
L_{3}:=\{u:d_{r}(u)>d_{b}(u)+1\}
. The number of red edges is

(43)

e_{r}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{u\in L_{1}}d_{r}(u)+\sum_{u\in L_{2}}d_{r}(u)+\sum_{u\in L_{3}}d_{r}(u)\right)\,.

If
L_{3}=\emptyset
then the assertion is immediate since

e_{r}(\varphi)\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in L_{1}\cup L_{2}}(d_{b}(u)+1)=e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n}{2}\,,

so we may assume
L_{3}\neq\emptyset
. Fix
u\in L_{3}
and fix
v\in N_{r}(u)
. Since the red neighborhood of every vertex is a blue clique, we have
d_{r}(v)\leqslant d_{b}(u)+1<d_{r}(u)\leqslant d_{b}(v)+1
, implying
v\in L_{1}
and

(44)

d_{r}(u)+d_{r}(v)\leqslant d_{b}(u)+d_{b}(v)+2\,.

We claim there exists an injection
f:L_{3}\to L_{1}
such that
f(u)\in N_{r}(u)
for all
u\in L_{3}
. Notice that this is sufficient to prove the inequality
e_{r}(\varphi)\leqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n}{2}
for the following reasons. For all
u\in L_{3}
, since
d_{r}(u)>d_{b}(u)+1
and
f(u)\in N_{r}(u)
,
Equation

44
implies

(45)

d_{r}(u)+d_{r}(f(u))\leqslant d_{b}(u)+d_{b}(f(u))+2\,.

The definition of
f
,
Equation

43
, and
Equation

45
then imply

\displaystyle e_{r}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})}d_{r}(u)+\sum_{u\in L_{2}}d_{r}(u)+\sum_{u\in L_{3}}d_{r}(u)+\sum_{u\in f(L_{3})}d_{r}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})}d_{r}(u)+\sum_{u\in L_{2}}(d_{b}(u)+1)+\sum_{u\in L_{3}}(d_{r}(u)+d_{r}(f(u)))\right)

(46)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})}(d_{b}(u)+1)+\sum_{u\in L_{2}}(d_{b}(u)+1)+\sum_{u\in L_{3}}(d_{b}(u)+d_{b}(f(u))+2)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in V(K_{n})}(d_{b}(u)+1)=e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n}{2}\,.

It remains to establish that such
f
exists. Let
w_{1},\dots,w_{l}
be an arbitrary ordering of the vertices in
L_{3}
, and let
W_{i}:=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{i}\}
. We will prove by induction on
i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
that there exists an injection
g_{i}:W_{i}\to L_{1}
such that
g_{i}(u)\in N_{r}(u)
for all
u\in W_{i}
. The claim is clearly true for
i=1
since we may set
g_{1}(w_{1})
to be any member of
N_{r}(w_{1})
. Assume
i\geqslant 2
and assume the claim holds for all smaller values of
i
. Notice that there must exist at least two distinct vertices in the set
U_{i}:=N_{r}(w_{i})\setminus g_{i-1}(W_{i-1})
. Indeed, we have
N_{r}(w_{i})\subseteq g_{i-1}(W_{i-1})\cup U_{i}
and

B:=g_{i-1}^{-1}(N_{r}(w_{i})\setminus U_{i})\subseteq N_{b}(w_{i})\,,

since if the red neighborhoods of two vertices overlap then those two vertices are joined by a blue edge. If
|U_{i}|\leqslant 1
then by injectivity of
g_{i-1}
it holds that

d_{b}(w_{i})=|N_{b}(w_{i})|\geqslant|B|=|N_{r}(w_{i})|-|U_{i}|\geqslant d_{r}(w_{i})-1\,,

contradicting our assumption that
w_{i}\in L_{3}
, proving our claim that
|U_{i}|\geqslant 2
. Fixing
x\in U_{i}
, we can now define an injection

g_{i}(w_{j}):=\begin{cases}g_{i-1}(w_{j})&1\leqslant j\leqslant i-1\\
x&j=i\end{cases}\,,

completing the induction step. Hence
f:=g_{l}
satisfies the claimed properties, completing the proof of the inequality
e_{r}(\varphi)\leqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n}{2}
.
∎

The next lemma proves that equality holds in
(
9
)
if and only if
\varphi
has the the extremal structure
{\mathcal{E}}(n)
defined in
Section

3
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\varphi
be a red–green–blue coloring of
E(K_{n})
such that
\varphi
has no triangle colored (red, red, red) nor any colored (red, red, green). Then one has
e_{r}(\varphi)=e_{b}(\varphi)+\lfloor n/2\rfloor
if and only if
\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}(n)
.

Proof.

If
\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}(n)
then clearly
(
9
)
holds with equality. Conversely, suppose equality is attained, and first assume
n
is even. We claim that
d_{r}(u)=d_{b}(u)+1
for all
u\in V(K_{n})
, which immediately implies
\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}(n)
. Let
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
, and
L_{3}
be as defined in the proof of
Lemma

3.2
. Since
n
is even and equality holds in
Equation

46
, the set
L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})
must be empty. We claim that
L_{3}=\emptyset
: indeed, the proof of
Lemma

3.2
shows that at each step
i
of the induction, the set
U_{i}
contains at least two distinct elements; if it were the case that
L_{3}\neq\emptyset
then

U_{l}\setminus\{f(w_{l})\}=N_{r}(w_{l})\setminus f(W_{l})\subseteq L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})

would be nonempty. All vertices
u\in V(K_{n})
thus have
u\in L_{2}
and
d_{r}(u)=d_{b}(u)+1
.

Suppose equality is attained in
(
9
)
and
n
is odd. We may assume there is no vertex
u
for which
d_{g}(u)=n-1
: if there were one then it could be removed while maintaining equality in
(
9
)
, leaving us in the case when
n
is even. We claim that
L_{1}\neq\emptyset
: if
L_{1}
were empty then the set
L_{3}
must also be empty (by the argument in the previous paragraph), and all vertices
u\in V(G)
satisfy
d_{r}(u)=d_{b}(u)+1
, implying
n
is even. We may thus assume
L_{1}\neq\emptyset
. We claim that for all
u\in L_{1}
one has
d_{r}(u)=d_{b}(u)
. Indeed, suppose
d_{r}(u)\leqslant d_{b}(u)-1
for some
u\in L_{1}
. Invoking
Equation

45
, it follows that

\displaystyle e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n-1}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(d_{b}(u)+\sum_{v\in V(K_{n})\setminus\{u\}}(d_{b}(v)+1)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\left((d_{r}(u)+1)+\sum_{v\in V(K_{n})\setminus\{u\}}d_{r}(v)\right)=e_{r}(\varphi)+\frac{1}{2}\,,

contradicting the assumption of equality in
(
9
)
. Fix
u\in L_{1}
, which we have shown satisfies
d_{r}(u)=d_{b}(u)
. The graph
G:=K_{n}-u
has an even number of vertices and, for any bijection
\sigma:V(K_{n-1})\to V(G)
, the coloring
\psi:=\varphi\circ\sigma
of
E(K_{n-1})
meets
(
9
)
with equality. Hence the analysis for the case when
n
is even implies
\psi\in{\mathcal{E}}(n-1)
, which is to say there is a partition
V(K_{n-1})=H_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup H_{k}
in which
H_{i}=A_{i}\sqcup B_{i}
and
|A_{i}|=|B_{i}|
for all
i\in[k]
. Notice that the only manner whereby
d_{r}(u)=d_{b}(u)
can be satisfied in
\varphi
is if there exists
i
such that
N_{r}(u)\in\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
and
\varphi(uv)
is green for all
v\not\in H_{i}
, which is precisely the assertion that
\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}(n)
, completing the proof.
∎

The following lemma is the main technical assertion invoked in the proof of
Proposition

3.3
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
and let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
be even. Let
\varphi
be a red–green–blue coloring of
E(K_{n})
such that there is no triangle colored (red, red, red) nor any colored (red, red, green). Assume
e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n}{2}-\alpha n^{2}
. Let
\eta\geqslant 2\sqrt{2\alpha}
and assume there exist at least
\eta n
vertices
v
with
d_{r}(v)>\eta n
. Then there exist disjoint subsets
X,Y,Z\subseteq V(K_{n})
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

the edges
E(X)\cup E(Y)
are all colored blue,

(2)

|X|=|Y|
,
|X\cup Y\cup Z|\geqslant\eta n
, and
|Z|\leqslant 5(2\alpha)^{1/4}n
,

(3)

letting
S:=X\cup Y\cup Z
, it holds that
e_{r}(S,V(K_{n})\setminus S)\leqslant 8\sqrt{2\alpha}n^{2}
,

(4)

letting
U:=V(K_{n})\setminus S
, it holds that
e_{r}(U)\geqslant e_{b}(U)+\lfloor|U|/2\rfloor-(\alpha+8\sqrt{2\alpha})n^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
, and
L_{3}
be as defined in the proof of
Lemma

3.2
. As shown in the proof of
Lemma

8.1
, we may assume
L_{1}\neq\emptyset
: if
L_{1}
were empty then in fact
\varphi\in{\mathcal{E}}(n)
. Note it may be the case that
L_{3}=\emptyset
. Fix a (possibly empty) injection
f:L_{3}\to L_{1}
such that
f(u)\in N_{r}(u)
for all
u\in L_{3}
, as provided by the proof of
Lemma

3.2
. Let

\displaystyle D_{r}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})}d_{r}(u)+\sum_{u\in L_{3}}(d_{r}(u)+d_{r}(f(u)))\right)\,,

\displaystyle D_{b}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})}(d_{b}(u)+1)+\sum_{u\in L_{3}}(d_{b}(u)+d_{b}(f(u))+2)\right)\,,

and define the sets

\displaystyle M_{1}

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3}):d_{r}(u)\geqslant d_{b}(u)+1-\sqrt{2\alpha}n\big\}\,,

\displaystyle M_{3}

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in L_{3}:d_{r}(u)+d_{r}(f(u))\geqslant d_{b}(u)+d_{b}(f(u))+2-\sqrt{2\alpha}n\big\}\,.

By our assumption that
e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n}{2}-\alpha n^{2}
and by
(
46
)
we have
D_{b}-\alpha n^{2}\leqslant D_{r}\leqslant D_{b}
, which implies

(47)

|(L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3}))\setminus M_{1}|+|L_{3}\setminus M_{3}|\leqslant\sqrt{2\alpha}n\,.

For all
u\in M_{3}
, using the fact that
f(u)\in L_{1}
, we have

(48)

\displaystyle 0\leqslant d_{b}(f(u))+1-d_{r}(u)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{2\alpha}n\,,

\displaystyle 0\leqslant d_{b}(u)+1-d_{r}(f(u))

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{2\alpha}n\,,

since otherwise we would contradict the definition of
M_{3}
. Let
U:=M_{1}\cup f(M_{3})\cup L_{2}\cup M_{3}
and note that
(
47
)
implies
|U|\geqslant(1-2\sqrt{2\alpha})n
. Make the following definitions

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle:=\argmax\{d_{r}(u):u\in U\}\,,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle:=\argmax\{d_{r}(u):u\in N_{r}(x)\cap U\}\,,

where
\argmax
selects any vertex
u
achieving the maximum. Define also the vertex subsets
X:=N_{r}(y)
,
Y^{\prime}:=N_{r}(x)
, and
S:=X\cup Y^{\prime}
. Note that by the hypotheses of the lemma,
d_{r}(x)>2\sqrt{2\alpha}n
, so
N_{r}(x)\cap U
is nonempty and
y
is well-defined.

Letting
\beta:=8\sqrt{2\alpha}
, we claim that

(49)

e_{r}(S,\overline{S})\leqslant\beta n^{2}\,,

where
\overline{S}:=V(G)\setminus S
. Suppose that instead the opposite of
(
49
)
held. Then at least one of the two inequalities
e_{r}(X,\overline{S})>\beta n^{2}/2
or
e_{r}(Y^{\prime},\overline{S})>\beta n^{2}/2
must hold. We will show both cases lead to a contradiction.

Case 1.

First assume
e_{r}(X,\overline{S})>\beta n^{2}/2
. Let
W:=\bigcup E_{r}(X,\overline{S})
be the vertex set covered by the edges
E_{r}(X,\overline{S})
, and define the bipartite graph
J:=(W,E_{r}(X,\overline{S}))
. Since
J
has at least
\beta n^{2}/2
edges, it contains a subgraph
J^{\prime}
with minimum degree
>\beta n/2
(see e.g.
[
24
, p. 6]
). In particular
|X\cap W|>\beta n/2
. Notice that
2\sqrt{2\alpha}n<\beta n/2
, hence
U\cap X\cap W
contains at least one vertex, say
w
. Notice that
y\in N_{r}(w)
and
d_{r}(w,W)\geqslant\beta n/2
, so the fact that
N_{r}(w)
is a blue clique implies
d_{b}(y)+1\geqslant d_{r}(x)+\beta n/2
. We consider the following three mutually exclusive cases.

Case 1a.

If
y\in M_{1}\cup L_{2}
then we compute

d_{r}(y)\geqslant d_{b}(y)+1-\sqrt{2\alpha}n\geqslant d_{r}(x)+\frac{\beta n}{2}-\sqrt{2\alpha}n>d_{r}(x)\,,

contradicting that
x
was chosen with maximum red degree.

Case 1b.

If
y\in f(M_{3})
then there exists
u\in M_{3}
such that
y=f(u)
. The inequalities in
(
48
)
then imply
d_{r}(u)\geqslant d_{b}(y)+1-\sqrt{2\alpha}n
, and we deduce similarly to Case 1a that
d_{r}(u)>d_{r}(x)
, a contradiction.

Case 1c.

If
y\in M_{3}
then
d_{r}(y)>d_{b}(y)+1\geqslant d_{r}(x)
(since
y\in N_{r}(x)
), a contradiction.

Case 2.

Now assume
e_{r}(Y^{\prime},\overline{S})>\beta n^{2}/2
. First note
y\not\in M_{3}
: if
y\in M_{3}
then by the proof of
Lemma

3.2
, there exists a choice of the mapping
f
such that
x=f(y)
, which implies
d_{r}(y)>d_{r}(x)
, instantiating a contradiction. As in Case 1, there exists a vertex
w\in Y^{\prime}
such that
d_{r}(w,\overline{S})>\beta n/2
. Since
x\in N_{r}(w)
and
N_{r}(w)
is a blue clique, it follows that
d_{b}(x)+1>d_{r}(y)+\beta n/2
, and we will use this fact several times in what follows. We consider the following cases.

Case 2a.

Assume
x\in M_{1}\cup L_{2}
. First, if
y\in M_{1}\cup L_{2}
then we compute

\displaystyle d_{r}(x)

\displaystyle\geqslant d_{b}(x)+1-\sqrt{2\alpha}n>d_{r}(y)+\frac{\beta n}{2}-\sqrt{2\alpha}n

\displaystyle\geqslant d_{b}(y)+1+\frac{\beta n}{2}-2\sqrt{2\alpha}n\geqslant d_{r}(x)+\frac{\beta n}{2}-2\sqrt{2\alpha}n>d_{r}(x)\,,

where we used
2\sqrt{2\alpha}n<\beta n/2
, but this sequence of inequalities is impossible. Hence we may assume
y\in f(M_{3})
(since it was noted
y\not\in M_{3}
). Then there exists
z\in N_{r}(y,M_{3})
such that
y=f(z)
, hence
(
48
)
and the fact that
x\in M_{1}\cup L_{2}
imply

\displaystyle d_{r}(z)

\displaystyle\geqslant d_{b}(y)+1-\sqrt{2\alpha}n\geqslant d_{r}(x)-\sqrt{2\alpha}n\geqslant d_{b}(x)+1-2\sqrt{2\alpha}n

\displaystyle>d_{r}(y)+\frac{\beta n}{2}-2\sqrt{2\alpha}n=d_{r}(y)+2\sqrt{2\alpha}n\,,

thus again by
(
48
)
, we have that

(50)

d_{b}(z)+1\leqslant d_{r}(y)+\sqrt{2\alpha}n<d_{r}(z)-\sqrt{2\alpha}n\,.

Since
z\in L_{3}
, it holds that
N_{r}(z)\subseteq L_{1}
(as noted in the proof of
Lemma

3.2
). We let
N_{b}[z]:=N_{b}(z)\cup\{z\}
and claim

(51)

K:=N_{r}(z)\setminus f(N_{b}[z]\cap L_{3})\subseteq L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})\,.

Indeed, if there were a vertex
z^{\prime}\in K
not contained in
L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})
, then
z^{\prime}\in f(L_{3})
; hence there exists
x^{\prime}\in L_{3}
such that
z^{\prime}=f(x^{\prime})\in N_{r}(x^{\prime})
. Now since
x^{\prime},\,z\in N_{r}(z^{\prime})
the edge
(x^{\prime},z)
must be blue, but this contradicts the definition of
K
, namely that
f(x^{\prime})\not\in K
.

Using
(
51
)
, we compute

\displaystyle|L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})|

\displaystyle\geqslant|N_{r}(z)\setminus f(N_{b}[z]\cap L_{3})|

\displaystyle\geqslant|N_{r}(z)|-|f(N_{b}[z]\cap L_{3})|

\displaystyle\geqslant d_{r}(z)-|N_{b}[z]|

\displaystyle=d_{r}(z)-(d_{b}(z)+1)

\displaystyle>\sqrt{2\alpha}n\,,

where the third inequality holds by injectivity of
f
and the last inequality uses
(
50
)
. Note that for all
y^{\prime}\in N_{r}(z)
it holds that
d_{r}(y^{\prime})\leqslant d_{b}(z)+1
and
d_{r}(z)\leqslant d_{b}(y^{\prime})+1
, so together with
(
50
)
we deduce
d_{b}(y^{\prime})+1\geqslant d_{r}(y^{\prime})+\sqrt{2\alpha}n
. Using that
|K|>\sqrt{2\alpha}n
, we compute

\displaystyle D_{b}-D_{r}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})}(d_{b}(u)+1)-\sum_{u\in L_{1}\setminus f(L_{3})}d_{r}(u)\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in K}(d_{b}(u)+1-d_{r}(u))>\alpha n^{2}\,,

contradicting our assumption that
D_{r}\geqslant D_{b}-\alpha n^{2}
.

Case 2b.

If
x\in f(M_{3})\cup M_{3}
then by
(
48
)
there exists
u\in N_{r}(x)
such that

d_{r}(u)\geqslant d_{b}(x)+1-\sqrt{2\alpha}n\geqslant d_{r}(y)+\frac{\beta n}{2}-\sqrt{2\alpha}n>d_{r}(y)\,,

contradicting that
y
has maximum degree among the vertices in
N_{r}(x)
.

Hence we have proven
(
49
)
. If
|X|=|Y^{\prime}|
then set
Y:=Y^{\prime}
and
Z:=\emptyset
. If
|X|<|Y^{\prime}|
then we will remove vertices from
Y^{\prime}
to obtain a set of equal size as
X
, which is done as follows. Let
k:=|X|
and
l:=|Y^{\prime}|-|X|
. Using
(
49
)
and applying
Lemma

3.2
to the coloring
\varphi
restricted to
\overline{S}
, we compute

\displaystyle e_{r}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=e_{r}(S,\overline{S})+e_{r}(X,Y^{\prime})+e_{r}(\overline{S})\leqslant\beta n^{2}+k(k+l)+e_{b}(\overline{S})+\frac{n-2k-l}{2}\,,

\displaystyle e_{b}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\geqslant e_{b}(X)+e_{b}(Y^{\prime})+e_{b}(\overline{S})

\displaystyle\geqslant\binom{k}{2}+\binom{k+l}{2}+e_{b}(\overline{S})=k(k+l)+\frac{l(l-1)}{2}-k+e_{b}(\overline{S})\,,

which, together with the inequality
e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+n/2-\alpha n^{2}
, quickly implies the bound
l\leqslant\sqrt{2(\alpha+\beta)}n\leqslant 5(2\alpha)^{1/4}n
, as claimed. Let
Z\subseteq Y^{\prime}
be any subset with
|Z|=l
, and define
Y:=Y^{\prime}\setminus Z
.

By construction, the edges
E(X)\cup E(Y)
are all colored blue,
|X|=|Y|
, and
|S|>\eta n
(since
d_{r}(x)>\eta n
). Defining
U:=V(K_{n})\setminus S
and applying
Lemma

3.2
to
S
, we compute

\displaystyle e_{r}(U)

\displaystyle\geqslant e_{r}(\varphi)-e_{r}(S)-\beta n^{2}\geqslant\left(e_{b}(\varphi)+\frac{n}{2}-\alpha n^{2}\right)-\left(e_{b}(S)+\left\lfloor\frac{|S|}{2}\right\rfloor\right)-\beta n^{2}

\displaystyle\geqslant e_{b}(U)+\left\lfloor\frac{n-|S|}{2}\right\rfloor-(\alpha+\beta)n^{2}=e_{b}(U)+\left\lfloor\frac{|U|}{2}\right\rfloor-(\alpha+\beta)n^{2}\,,

where the first inequality invokes
(
49
)
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.3
.

Define the function
f_{0}:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
by
f_{0}(x)=x
. For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
let
f_{k}:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
F_{k}:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}
be defined by

\displaystyle f_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{64}{\epsilon^{2}}\left(f_{k-1}(x)+8\sqrt{2f_{k-1}(x)}\right)\,,

\displaystyle F_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\left(8\sqrt{2f_{l}(x)}+5(2f_{l}(x))^{1/4}\right)\,.

Clearly
f_{k}(0)=0
and
f_{k}
is strictly increasing for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
(since it is a sum and composition of such functions). Hence, letting
N:=\lceil 16/\epsilon\rceil
, there exists
\delta_{0}\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
such that
f_{N}(\delta_{0})<\epsilon^{2}/2^{11}
. Similarly
F_{k}(0)=0
and
F_{k}
is strictly increasing for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, so there exists
\delta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1}{8})
such that
F_{N}(\delta_{1})<\epsilon/4
.

We claim
\delta:=\min\{\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\epsilon^{2}/64\}
suffices. So assume
e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\lfloor n/2\rfloor-\delta n^{2}
, and we claim
d(\varphi,{\mathcal{F}}(n))\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}
. If
n<8/\epsilon
then the assertion is immediate since
\delta n^{2}<1
and
Lemma

8.1
proves
\varphi\in{\mathcal{F}}(n)
. Henceforth we assume
n\geqslant 8/\epsilon
. We may further assume
n
is even: if
n
is odd then we can select any vertex
v
and edit each edge incident to
v
to be green; since
n\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}/8
, this operation expends at most
\epsilon n^{2}/8
Hamming distance; we may proceed with the coloring
\varphi
restricted to
K_{n}-v
, and show this coloring is within
7\epsilon n^{2}/8
Hamming distance of
{\mathcal{F}}(n)
.

We apply
Lemma

8.2
inductively in the following manner. In each application of
Lemma

8.2
we set
\eta:=\epsilon/16
, noting that by our choice of
\delta
, the inequality
\eta\geqslant 2\sqrt{2\alpha}
will always hold in the following steps. Let
\alpha_{0}:=\delta
,
\varphi_{0}:=\varphi
, and
R_{0}:=V(K_{n})
. For all
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
|R_{l-1}|\leqslant\epsilon n/8
or if
R_{l-1}
has less than
\epsilon n/16
vertices
u
with
d_{r}(u)>\epsilon n/16
(with respect to the coloring
\varphi_{l-1}
), then terminate the induction. Otherwise
|R_{l-1}|>\epsilon n/8
and fix an arbitrary bijection
\psi_{l}:V(K_{n_{l}})\to R_{l-1}
, where
n_{l}:=|R_{l-1}|
. Define the red–green–blue coloring
\varphi_{l}:=\varphi\circ\psi_{l}
of
E(K_{n_{l}})
. We then apply
Lemma

8.2
to the coloring
\varphi_{l}
with
\alpha=f_{l}(\delta)
, yielding vertex subsets
X_{l},Y_{l},Z_{l}\subseteq V(K_{n_{l}})
satisfying the conclusions of the lemma. Set
H_{l}:=\psi_{l}(X_{l}\cup Y_{l})
,
S_{l}:=H_{l}\cup\psi_{l}(Z_{l})
, and

R_{l}:=V(K_{n})\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{l}S_{j}\,.

Notice that
|S_{l}|>\epsilon n/16
for all
l
(by the minimum red degree condition), so the induction terminates after some finite number
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
of steps, where
k\leqslant N
.

Define the edge sets

\begin{array}[]{lll}\displaystyle A:=\bigcup_{l=1}^{k}(E(X_{l})\cup E(Y_{l}))\,,&B:=E_{b}(\varphi)\setminus A\,,&\displaystyle C:=\bigcup_{l=1}^{k}E(X_{l},Y_{l})\,,\\[20.0pt]
\displaystyle D:=\bigcup_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}E_{r}(S_{i},S_{j})\,,&\displaystyle E:=\bigcup_{l=1}^{k}E_{r}(Z_{l},V(K_{n}))\,,&F:=E_{r}(R_{k},V(K_{n}))\,,\end{array}

where the notations
E_{r}
and
E_{b}
in those definitions refer to the coloring
\varphi
. Let
\psi
be the coloring of
E(K_{n})
such that
\psi(e)
is blue if
e\in A
, red if
e\in C
, and green otherwise. Notice that

(52)

\{e\in E(K_{n}):\varphi(e)\neq\psi(e)\}\subseteq B\cup(C\setminus E_{r}(\varphi))\cup D\cup E\cup F\,,

so it suffices to show the set on the right hand side of
(
52
)
has size
\leqslant 7\epsilon n^{2}/8
. By choice of
\delta
and the bounds given by
Lemma

8.2
it holds that
|D\cup E|\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}/4
. Indeed, for all
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
we edited the (at most
f_{l}(\delta)n^{2}
) red edges
E_{r}(S_{l},R_{l})
of
\varphi
to be green; and edited all edges incident to
Z_{l}
to be green (there were at most
5(2f_{l}(\delta))^{1/4}n^{2}
such edges). By definition of the condition for terminating the induction, there are at most
\epsilon n^{2}/8
red edges incident to
R_{k}
, hence
|F|\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}/8
. To bound
|B|+|C\setminus E_{r}(\varphi)|
, we first compute

\displaystyle e_{r}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\leqslant|D\cup E\cup F|+\sum_{l=1}^{k}e_{r}(X_{l},Y_{l})\leqslant\frac{3\epsilon}{8}n^{2}+\sum_{l=1}^{k}e_{r}(X_{l},Y_{l})

\displaystyle e_{b}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\geqslant|A|+|B|\,.

Noticing that
|A|+\lfloor n/2\rfloor\geqslant\sum_{l=1}^{k}e_{r}(X_{l},Y_{l})+|C\setminus E_{r}(\varphi)|
, and invoking
e_{r}(\varphi)\geqslant e_{b}(\varphi)+\lfloor n/2\rfloor-\delta n^{2}
, we compute that

\left(\frac{3\epsilon}{8}+\delta\right)n^{2}\geqslant|A|+\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor-\sum_{l=1}^{k}e_{r}(X_{l},Y_{l})+|B|\geqslant|B|+|C\setminus E_{r}(\varphi)|\,,

so using that
\delta<\epsilon/8
, we obtain
|B|+|C\setminus E_{r}(\varphi)|\leqslant\epsilon n^{2}/2
, completing the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Auxiliary Facts

Lemma A.1

(
[
11
, p. 5]
)
.

Let
n,m\geqslant 1
be integers. The following statements are valid.

(1)

If
j
is an integer such that
j\leqslant m\leqslant n
then

\binom{n-j}{m-j}{\binom{n}{m}}^{-1}\leqslant e^{-(1-m/n)j}\,.

(2)

If
j
is an integer such that
j\geqslant-m
and
m\leqslant n-\max\{1,j\}
then

\binom{n}{m+j}\binom{n}{m}^{-1}\leqslant\left(\frac{n-m}{m}\right)^{j}\leqslant e^{-\big(1-\frac{n-m}{m}\big)j}\,.

(3)

If
j,k
are nonnegative integers such that
m+k\leqslant n-j
then

\binom{n-j}{m+k}\binom{n}{m}^{-1}\leqslant\left(\frac{n-m}{n}\right)^{j}\left(\frac{n-m}{m}\right)^{k}\,.

Lemma A.2

(Convergence of hypergeometric to binomial)
.

Let
n,m,k,l
be nonnegative integers such that
k\leqslant n
and
l\leqslant m\leqslant n+\min\{0,k-l\}
. If
m=\Theta(n)
,
k=O(\log n)
, and
l=O(\log n)
as
n\to\infty
then

\binom{n-k}{m-l}\binom{n}{m}^{-1}\sim\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{l}\left(1-\frac{m}{n}\right)^{k-l}\,.

Lemma A.3
.

If
G
is a graph on
n
vertices and
m
edges then
G
has a matching of size at least
\lfloor\min\{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\sqrt{m},\frac{m}{n}\}\rfloor
. Hence if
m=\alpha n^{2}
then
G
has a matching of size at least
\frac{1}{8}\alpha n
.

Proof.

If
0\leqslant m\leqslant\max\{n,7\}
then the assertion is obvious, so we assume
m>\max\{n,7\}
(which implies
\min\{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\sqrt{m},\frac{m}{n}\}>1
). Let
\nu(G)
denote the size of a maximum matching in
G
.
[
27
]
proved in
[
27
]
that every graph
H
on
k
vertices with
\nu:=\nu(H)
satisfies

e(H)\leqslant\max\left\{\binom{2\nu+1}{2},(k-\nu)\nu+\binom{\nu}{2}\right\}\,.

Let
\mu:=\min\{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\sqrt{m},\frac{m}{n}\}
. Since
\mu\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(\sqrt{2m}-1)
, we have

m\geqslant 2\mu^{2}+2\mu-\frac{1}{2}>\frac{(2\mu+1)\cdot 2\mu}{2}\geqslant\binom{2\lfloor\mu\rfloor+1}{2}\,,

where the second inequality uses
\mu>1
. Since
x\mapsto(n-x)x+\frac{1}{2}x(x-1)
is strictly increasing for
x\in[1,\frac{n}{2}]
, and since (by completing the square)
n-\sqrt{n^{2}-2m}>\frac{m}{n}\geqslant\mu
, we have

(n-\lfloor\mu\rfloor)\lfloor\mu\rfloor+\binom{\lfloor\mu\rfloor}{2}\leqslant(n-\mu)\mu+\frac{\mu(\mu-1)}{2}\leqslant n\mu-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}<m\,.

It follows from the theorem of
[
27
]
that
\nu(G)\geqslant\lfloor\mu\rfloor
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Let
1\leqslant m\leqslant n
be integers and let
p:=m/n
. Let
S
be the uniformly random subset of
[n]
of size
m
, and let
T
be the random subset of
[n]
where each element is included independently with probability
p
. For any event
A\subseteq 2^{[n]}
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{S}\{A\}\leqslant\sqrt{8np}\cdot{\mathbb{P}}_{T}\{A\}\,.

Proof.

Using the fact that

\binom{n}{k}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{n}{8k(n-k)}}2^{H(k/n)n}\,,

for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
(e.g.
[
46
, p. 309]
), we compute that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{T}\{A\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}{\mathbb{P}}_{T}\{A\,|\,|T|=k\}{\mathbb{P}}_{T}\{|T|=k\}

\displaystyle\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{T}\{A\,|\,|T|=np\}{\mathbb{P}}_{T}\{|T|=np\}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}_{S}\{A\}\binom{n}{np}p^{np}(1-p)^{n(1-p)}

\displaystyle\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{S}\{A\}\sqrt{\frac{1}{8p(1-p)n}}2^{H(p)n}p^{np}(1-p)^{n(1-p)}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}_{S}\{A\}\sqrt{\frac{1}{8p(1-p)n}}\,,

completing the proof.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Let
A\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
be a measurable set. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
. For all
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
let
Y_{ij}
be the indicator for the event
(X_{i},X_{j})\in A
. For all
\delta>0
the random variable
Y:=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}Y_{ij}
satisfies

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{Y\leqslant(1-\delta)|A|\binom{n}{2}\right\}\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}|A|^{2}}{32}\cdot n\right)\,.

Proof.

The function
f:[0,1]^{n}\to{\mathbb{N}}
defined by

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\bm{1}\{(x_{i},x_{j})\in A\}

satisfies the bounded differences condition with parameter
n
, that is, whenever two vectors
x,y\in[0,1]^{n}
differ on at most one coordinate, we have
|f(x)-f(y)|\leqslant n
. It follows that the martingale defined by
Z_{i}:={\mathbb{E}}[Y|X_{1},\dots,X_{i}]
for all
i\in[n]
is
n
-Lipschitz, that is,
|Z_{i+1}-Z_{i}|\leqslant n
for all
i
almost surely. Since
{\mathbb{E}}Y=|A|\binom{n}{2}
, the bounded differences inequality (e.g.
[
67
, Theorem 4.4.4]
) implies that for all
\lambda>0
,

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{Y\leqslant|A|\binom{n}{2}-\lambda\right\}\leqslant e^{-\lambda^{2}/(2n^{3})}\,,

and the result follows by taking
\lambda=\delta|A|\binom{n}{2}
.
∎

Appendix B
Deferred Proofs

Proof of
Lemma

2.9
.

Let
\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}
denote the set of graph limits of convergent sequences of graphs in
{\mathcal{Q}}(n)
. For all
\gamma\in(0,1)
let
\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\gamma}
be the set of all
W\in\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}
such that
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
. Letting
m_{\gamma}:=\lfloor\gamma\binom{n}{2}\rfloor
, we compute

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{Q}}(n)\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\sup_{\gamma\in[0,1]}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{Q}}(n,m_{\gamma})\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\sup_{\gamma\in[0,1]}\log_{2}\left(|{\mathcal{Q}}(n,m_{\gamma})|\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m_{\gamma}}(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sup_{\gamma\in[0,1]}\left\{\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}|{\mathcal{Q}}(n,m_{\gamma})|+\gamma\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)+\log_{2}(1-p)\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leqslant\sup_{\gamma\in[0,1]}\left\{\sup_{W\in\widehat{{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\gamma}}H(W)+\gamma\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)+\log_{2}(1-p)\right\}=\sup_{W\in\overline{{\mathcal{Q}}}}I_{p}(W)\,,

where we used
[
31
, Theorem 1]
to obtain the inequality.
∎

We will need the following lemma in the proof of
Proposition

2.10
; note that an analogous result was proven for triangles
[
64
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
p\in(0,1)
,
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. Fix a graph
H
and let
{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
be the set of induced-
H
-free graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges. Define the set

{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}:=\{W\in{\mathcal{W}}:t(K_{2},W)=\gamma,\,t_{\ind}(H,W)=0,\,\rand(W)>0\}

and assume
{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}\neq\emptyset
. Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
denote the set of graphons that are limits of convergent sequences of graphs in
{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
. Then
{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
and

(53)

\sup\{I_{p}(W):W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}\}=\sup\{I_{p}(W):W\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}\}\,.

Proof.

Let
W\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}
and we claim there exists a sequence
G_{n}\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
of graphs such that
G_{n}\to W
. The sequence
G(n,W)
of random graphs (
Definition

2.8
) converges with probability 1 to
W
in cut metric by
[
15
, Theorem 4.5]
. For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
let
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{n}:=e(G(n,W))
. Hence
m^{\prime}\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
with probability 1. For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
X^{n}_{1},\dots,X^{n}_{n}
and
Y^{n}_{ij}
,
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
, be the random variables that are i.i.d. uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
and that are used to sample
G(n,W)
as in
Definition

2.8
. For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
let
Y_{n}
denote the number of pairs
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
such that
(X^{n}_{i},X^{n}_{j})\in R:=R_{W}
. Applying
Lemma

A.5
with
\delta=1/2
, the random variable
Y_{n}
satisfies

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{Y_{n}\leqslant\frac{|R|n^{2}}{8}\right\}\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}|R|^{2}}{32}\cdot n\right)\,.

By the Borel–Cantelli lemma, there are only finitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
Y_{n}\leqslant|R|n^{2}/8
with probability 1. Whenever
Y_{n}>|R|n^{2}/8
, there exist
c:=|R|n^{2}/8
edges
\{i_{1},j_{1}\},\dots,\{i_{c},j_{c}\}
such that
(X^{n}_{i_{k}},X^{n}_{j_{k}})\in R
for all
k\in[c]
; in this case Chernoff’s inequality implies that the random variable
Z_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{c}Y^{n}_{i_{k}j_{k}}
satisfies

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{|Z_{n}-c/2|\geqslant n/4\right\}\leqslant 2e^{-n/32}\,.

Again by the Borel–Cantelli lemma, with probability 1 there are only finitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that both
Y_{n}>|R|n^{2}/8
and
|Z_{n}-c/2|\geqslant n/4
. For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
define the random graph
H_{n}
on the vertex set
[n]
as follows:

(1)

If
m^{\prime}=m
then let
H_{n}=G
.

(2)

If any of the following three conditions holds, then let
H_{n}
be an arbitrary graph in
{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
: (1)
Y_{n}\leqslant|R|n^{2}/8
; (2)
Y_{n}>|R|n^{2}/8
and
|Z_{n}-c/2|\geqslant n/4
; or (3)
Y_{n}>|R|n^{2}/8
and
|Z_{n}-c/2|<n/4
and
|m-m^{\prime}|>c/2-n/4
.

(3)

Otherwise,
Y_{n}>|R|n^{2}/8
and
|Z_{n}-c/2|<n/4
and
d:=|m-m^{\prime}|\leqslant c/2-n/4
. In this case there exist edge sets
E_{1}=\{i_{k}j_{k}:k\in[d]\}
and
E_{2}=\{i_{k}^{\prime}j_{k}^{\prime}:k\in[d]\}
such that
E_{1}\subseteq E(G)
,
E_{2}\subseteq E(\overline{G})
, and
(X_{u},X_{v})\in R_{W}
for all
uv\in E_{1}\cup E_{2}
. If
m>m^{\prime}
then let
H_{n}=(V,E(G)\cup E_{2})
; otherwise
m<m^{\prime}
, and let
H_{n}=(V,E(G)\setminus E_{1})
.

By definition, the graph
H_{n}
always belongs to
{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
, and the conditions of case (
ii
) are met for only finitely many
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with probability 1. It follows that
H_{n}\to W
in cut metric with probability 1, completing the proof of the inclusion
{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
.

To prove the second part of the lemma, first note that the supremum of
I_{p}(W)
over
{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
is achieved only if
\rand(W)>0
. Indeed, if
W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
is a graphon with
\rand(W)=0
then
I_{p}(W)=\gamma\log_{2}(\frac{p}{1-p})+\log_{2}(1-p)
, and if
W^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
is a graphon with
r=:\rand(W^{\prime})>0
(such a graphon exists by the first part of the lemma) then
H(W^{\prime})>0
and we have

I_{p}(W^{\prime})=H(W^{\prime})+\gamma\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)+\log_{2}(1-p)>I_{p}(W)\,.

The result follows if we prove
\{W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}:\rand(W)>0\}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}
. Indeed,
W
is the limit of a convergent sequence
G\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,m)
of graphs, and since
\rand(W)>0
, we know
v(G_{n})\to\infty
. Hence continuity of the homomorphism density implies
t(F,\Gamma)=\lim_{n\to\infty}t(F,G_{n})
for all fixed graphs
F
, in particular,
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
and
t_{\ind}(K_{1,3},W)=0
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.10
.

For all
\gamma\in(0,1)
let
{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}
denote the set of all
W\in{\mathcal{F}}
with
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
. Fix
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that there exists
W\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}
with
\rand(W)>0
, and let
G\sim G(n,W)
. Letting
m_{\gamma}:=\lfloor\gamma\binom{n}{2}\rfloor
, we claim

(54)

H(G)\leqslant\log_{2}|{\mathcal{F}}(n,m_{\gamma},H)|+o(n^{2})\,,

where
H(G)
is the entropy of
G
as a discrete random variable. For all
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
let
{\mathcal{S}}(n)
be the set of all
H\in{\mathcal{G}}(n)
such that
{\mathbb{P}}\{G=H\}>0
, and let
{\mathcal{S}}(n,m):={\mathcal{S}}(n)\cap{\mathcal{G}}(n,m)
. We write
{\mathbb{P}}\{H^{\prime}\}:={\mathbb{P}}\{G=H^{\prime}\}
and
{\mathbb{P}}\{{\mathcal{H}}\}:={\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{H}}\}
for a graph
H^{\prime}
and set of graphs
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Let
0<L<U<1
be fixed constants such that the set
R:=\{(x,y):L<W(x,y)<U\}
has positive measure. For all
\epsilon\in(0,1)
let
R_{\epsilon}\subseteq R
be a fixed subset of measure
\epsilon|R|
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, and
Y_{ij}
,
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
, denote the random variables associated with
G(n,W)
(see
Definition

2.8
). Let

Y:=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\bm{1}\{(X_{i},X_{j})\in R_{\epsilon}\}\hskip 17.07164pt\text{and}\hskip 17.07164ptZ:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n\\
(X_{i},X_{j})\in R_{\epsilon}\end{subarray}}\bm{1}\{Y_{ij}\leqslant W(X_{i},X_{j})\}\,.

By
Lemma

A.5
,
Y
is concentrated around its mean
y_{\epsilon}:=|R_{\epsilon}|\binom{n}{2}
. Conditioned on
Y
, the random variable
Z
also concentrates around its mean
LY\leqslant z_{\epsilon}\leqslant UY
by Chernoff’s inequality. Let
\delta=\delta(\epsilon)>0
be sufficiently small and let

{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n):=\left\{H\in{\mathcal{S}}(n):{\mathbb{P}}\left\{H\,\bigg|\,\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{0}{y_\epsilon-\delta{\textstyle\binom{n}{2}}\leq Y\leq y_\epsilon+\delta{\textstyle\binom{n}{2}}\vphantom{\displaystyle\bigcup}}{z_\epsilon-\delta{\textstyle\binom{n}{2}}\leq Z\leq z_\epsilon+\delta{\textstyle\binom{n}{2}}}\right\}>0\right\}

and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m):={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n)\cap{\mathcal{G}}(n,m)
. Let
\eta:=\epsilon|R|\min\{L,1-U\}
and we claim that for all
(\gamma-\eta)\binom{n}{2}\leqslant m\leqslant(\gamma+\eta)\binom{n}{2}
,

(55)

\log|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)|\leqslant\log|{\mathcal{S}}(n,m_{\gamma})|+o(n^{2})\,.

Indeed, for all
H\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)
, there exist numbers
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in[0,1]
and
y_{ij}\in[0,1]
in the support of
G
(in the sense of
Definition

2.8
) such that the number of pairs
(x_{i},x_{j})\in R_{\epsilon}
is in the interval
y_{\epsilon}\pm\delta\binom{n}{2}
and the number of
y_{ij}
such that
y_{ij}\leqslant W(x_{i},x_{j})
is in the interval
z_{\epsilon}\pm\delta\binom{n}{2}
. If
m<\gamma\binom{n}{2}
then we can obtain a graph
H^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}(n,m_{\gamma})
in the support of
G
by changing at most
\delta\binom{n}{2}
of the values
y_{ij}
from belonging to the interval
(W(x_{i},x_{j}),1)
to belonging to the interval
(0,W(x_{i},x_{j}))
, and analogously if
m>\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. Since
|R_{\epsilon}|=\epsilon|R|<\epsilon
, the mapping
f:{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)\to{\mathcal{S}}(n,m_{\gamma}):H\mapsto H^{\prime}
has the following property: for all
H^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}(n,m_{\gamma})
there are at most
2^{\epsilon\binom{n}{2}}
graphs
H\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)
such that
f(H)=H^{\prime}
. It directly follows that

|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)|\leqslant|{\mathcal{S}}(n,m_{\gamma})|2^{\epsilon\binom{n}{2}}\,,

completing the proof of
(
55
)
.

Since
e(G)\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\to\gamma
with probability 1, we use
(
55
)
to compute that

\displaystyle H(G)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{m=0}^{\binom{n}{2}}{\mathbb{P}}\{{\mathcal{S}}(n,m)\}\sum_{H^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}(n)}{\mathbb{P}}\{H^{\prime}\,|\,{\mathcal{S}}(n,m)\}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{H^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=-(1+o(1))\sum_{m=(\gamma-\eta)\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+\eta)\binom{n}{2}}{\mathbb{P}}\{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)\}\sum_{H^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}(n)}{\mathbb{P}}\{H^{\prime}\,|\,{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)\}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{H^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+o(1))\log_{2}\left(\sum_{m=(\gamma-\eta)\binom{n}{2}}^{(\gamma+\eta)\binom{n}{2}}|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(n,m)|\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+o(1))\log_{2}|{\mathcal{S}}(n,m_{\gamma})|\leqslant(1+o(1))\log_{2}|{\mathcal{F}}(n,m_{\gamma},H)|\,,

where we used concavity of the entropy, completing the proof of
(
54
)
. Using
(
54
)
and the fact that
H(G)\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\to H(W)
(see
[
33
, Theorem D.5]
), we obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,H)\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\geqslant\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}{\mathbb{P}}\{G\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,m_{\gamma},H)\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}\left(|{\mathcal{F}}(n,m_{\gamma},H)|\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)^{m_{\gamma}}(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\geqslant\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\left(H(G(n,W))+m_{\gamma}\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)\right)+\log_{2}(1-p)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=H(W)+\gamma\log_{2}\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)+\log_{2}(1-p)=I_{p}(W)\,,

and taking the supremum over
W\in{\mathcal{F}}
on the right-hand side proves the first inequality.

To prove the opposite inequality first let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}
be the set of graphons
W
that are limits of convergent sequences
G_{n}\in{\mathcal{F}}(n,H)
such that
t(K_{2},W)=\gamma
. Now
Lemma

B.1
proves

(56)

\sup_{W\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}}H(W)=\sup_{W\in{\mathcal{X}}_{\gamma}}H(W)\,.

so the upper bound follows directly by applying
Lemma

2.9
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.11
.

Let
W\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\gamma}
and
G\sim G(n,W)
. The proof of
Proposition

2.10
shows
H(G)\leqslant\log_{2}|{\mathcal{F}}(n,m,H)|+o(n^{2})
, so using the fact that
H(G)\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\to H(W)
,

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{n}{2}}\log_{2}|{\mathcal{F}}(n,m,H)|\geqslant\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(G)}{\binom{n}{2}}=H(W)\,.

Applying
Lemma

2.9
to
{\mathcal{F}}(n,m,H)
with
p=1/2
, and again using
Lemma

B.1
, we obtain the opposite inequality, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

Define the Lagrangian

L(x,y,\lambda,\mu)=yH\left(\frac{c-x}{y}\right)+\lambda\left(x+y-1\right)+\mu(y-x)\,.

The equation
\nabla L=0
implies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L}{\partial x}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{c-x}{y}\right)-\log\left(1-\frac{c-x}{y}\right)+\lambda-\mu=0\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L}{\partial y}(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\log\left(1-\frac{c-x}{y}\right)+\lambda+\mu=0\,,

hence

(57)

\lambda=\log\left(1-\frac{c-x}{y}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{c-x}{y}\right)\,,\hskip 14.22636pt\mu=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{c-x}{y}\right)\,.

The Karush–Kuhn–Tucker (KKT) conditions assert that
\lambda(x+y-1)=0
and
\mu(y-x)=0
, and that the dual variables
\lambda
and
\mu
are nonpositive. From the KKT conditions we deduce
x=y
, since otherwise
\mu=0
, implying the strictly suboptimal objective value
f(x,y)=0
. In the remainder we take
x=y
and analyze the two cases
\lambda=0
and
\lambda<0
pertaining to the first KKT equation. If
\lambda=0
then
(
57
)
implies
(c-x)/y=(3-\sqrt{5})/2
, which further implies
x=y=\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{10}c
; in this case
f
takes the value

g_{1}(c):=\frac{5+\sqrt{5}}{10}H\left(\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\right)c\,.

Note that the inequality
x+y\leqslant 1
implies
\lambda=0
is feasible if and only if
0<c\leqslant(5-\sqrt{5})/4
. Alternatively if
\lambda<0
, then the KKT condition asserts
x+y=1
and
x=y=1/2
, and
f
takes the value
g_{2}(c):=\frac{1}{2}H(2c-1)
. It is easy to verify that
g_{1}(c)
is the line tangent to
g_{2}(c)
at
c=(5-\sqrt{5})/4
, which also proves the second statement of the lemma. Hence by strict concavity of
g_{2}(c)
we have
g_{1}(c)\geqslant g_{2}(c)
with equality if and only if
c=(5-\sqrt{5})/4
.
Equation

11
now follows since we proved that
\lambda<0
whenever
c>(5-\sqrt{5})/4
.
∎

Appendix C
Asymptotic Enumeration of Co-Bipartite Graphs

In this section we prove the following proposition, which gives a formula for the asymptotic number co-bipartite graphs at edge density between
\frac{1}{2}
and
1
.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
\gamma\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. If
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
then

|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|\sim\left(\frac{r+1}{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(2\gamma-1\right)^{k^{2}+rk}\right)\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}\,,

where
r=n\bmod{2}
.

Note that by taking
\gamma=3/4
in
Proposition

C.1
, the asymptotics of
\log|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|
match the asymptotics of
\log|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n)|
, which reflects the fact that almost all co-bipartite graphs have edge density approximately
3/4
. The proof of
Proposition

C.1
is standard based on methods of earlier works including
[
28
,
54
]
.

The
imbalance
of a bipartition
\{A,B\}
of
V=[n]
is defined to be
||A|-|B||
, and we say that a bipartition is
almost equitable
if its imbalance is at most
\sqrt{\log n}
. Define the set of 2-clique-covered graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges

\Cov_{n,m}:=\{(G,\{A,B\}):G\in{\mathcal{G}}(n,m),\,V=A\sqcup B,\,G[A]=K_{A},\,G[B]=K_{B}\}\,.

We show in
Lemma

C.2
that almost every
(G,\{A,B\})\in\Cov_{n,m}
is almost equitable. In
Lemmas

C.3
and
C.3.1
, we show that almost all co-bipartite graphs
G\in{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)
admit a unique 2-clique-cover. By enumerating almost-equitable 2-clique-covered graphs on
n
vertices and
m
edges, we obtain an asymptotic formula for
|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|
.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
\gamma\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. For almost all
(G,\{A,B\})\in\mathrm{Cov}_{n,m}
, the bipartition
\{A,B\}
is almost equitable, that is,

|\mathrm{Cov}_{n,m}|\sim\sum_{A,B}\binom{|A|\cdot|B|}{m-\binom{|A|}{2}-\binom{|B|}{2}}\,,

where the sum is over almost-equitable bipartitions
\{A,B\}
of
V
.

Proof.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
define the set

I_{n}:=\begin{cases}\{2j:j\in{\mathbb{N}},\,\sqrt{\log n}<2j\leqslant n-2\}&n\text{ is even}\\
\{2j+1:j\in{\mathbb{N}},\,\sqrt{\log n}<2j+1\leqslant n-2\}&n\text{ is odd}\end{cases}\,,

so that
I_{n}
is the set of possible imbalances of a bipartition of
n
vertices that are
not
almost equitable. Using
Lemma

A.1
and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
, we find that for
k\in I_{n}
,

\displaystyle\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}{\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor\cdot\lceil n/2\rceil}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor-\lfloor k^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-(n^{2}+k^{2})/4+n/2}{\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}^{-1}

(58)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor-\lfloor k^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor-\lfloor k^{2}/4\rfloor+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\exp\left(-\left(1-\frac{m-\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}\right)\left\lfloor\frac{k^{2}}{4}\right\rfloor\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\sim\exp\left(-2(1-\gamma)\left\lfloor\frac{k^{2}}{4}\right\rfloor\right)<e^{-\frac{1}{4}(1-\gamma)k^{2}}\,,

provided
n
is large enough. Hence the number of
(G,\{A,B\})\in\mathrm{Cov}_{n,m}
where
\{A,B\}
is not almost equitable is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in I_{n}}\binom{n}{(n-k)/2}\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\sum_{k\in I_{n}}\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\left(\sum_{k=\lfloor\sqrt{\log n}\rfloor}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{1}{4}(1-\gamma)k^{2}}\right)\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor\cdot\lceil n/2\rceil}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\frac{n^{-4/(1-\gamma)^{2}}}{\sqrt{\log n}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor\cdot\lceil n/2\rceil}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}

(59)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leqslant\frac{n^{-4/(1-\gamma)^{2}}}{\sqrt{\log n}}\cdot|\mathrm{Cov}_{n,m}|\,,

where we used a standard Gaussian tail bound, and the last inequality holds since the second factor on the left-hand side of
(
59
)
is a lower bound on the number of 2-clique-covered graphs
(G,\{A,B\})\in\mathrm{Cov}_{n,m}
satisfying
||A|-|B||\leqslant 1
.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\gamma\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. Let
0\leqslant k\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\gamma-1/2}\cdot n
be an integer and let
\{A,B\}
be a bipartition of
V
with imbalance
k
. Let
G
be a uniformly random graph on
n
vertices and
m
edges conditioned on the event that
G[A]
and
G[B]
are cliques. The probability that
G
has a universal vertex, i.e. one adjacent to all other vertices, is at most
e^{-Cn}
for a constant
C=C(\gamma)>0
and large enough
n
.

Proof.

Since
\{A,B\}
has imbalance
k
, we may assume
|A|=(n-k)/2
and
|B|=(n+k)/2
. For vertices
v\in A
and
w\in B
, the inequality
|A|\leqslant|B|
implies
{\mathbb{P}}_{G}\{v\text{ is universal}\}
is at most
{\mathbb{P}}_{G}\{w\text{ is universal}\}
. The upper bound on
k
ensures that

m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}-\frac{n-k}{2}=m-\frac{n^{2}+k^{2}-2k}{4}\geqslant 0\,,

provided
n
is large enough. Using
Lemma

A.1
and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
, the probability that
w
is universal in
G
is at most

\displaystyle\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4-(n-k)/2}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}-(n-k)/2}\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\leqslant\exp\left(-\left(1-\frac{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}{(n-k)(n+k)/4}\right)\frac{n-k}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\exp\left(-\left(1-\frac{4m-n^{2}-k^{2}+2n}{n^{2}-k^{2}}\right)\frac{n^{2}-k^{2}}{2(n+k)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{n^{2}-2m-n}{2n}\right)\sim e^{-\frac{1}{2}(1-\gamma)n+\frac{1}{2}}\,.

It follows that the probability
G
contains a universal vertex is at most
n{\mathbb{P}}\{w\text{ is universal}\}\leqslant e^{-Cn}
for a constant
C=C(\gamma)>0
.
∎

Corollary C.3.1
.

Let
\gamma\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
m\sim\gamma\binom{n}{2}
. Almost all
G\in{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)
admit a unique covering by two cliques.

Proof.

Let
W\subseteq\mathrm{Cov}_{n,m}
denote the set of 2-clique-covered graphs
(G,\{A,B\})
on
n
vertices and
m
edges such that
\{A,B\}
is almost equitable and
G
has a unique 2-clique-cover. Let

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=\{(G,\{A,B\})\in\Cov_{n,m}\setminus W:||A|-|B||\leqslant\sqrt{\log n}\}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle:=\{(G,\{A,B\})\in\Cov_{n,m}\setminus W:||A|-|B||>\sqrt{\log n}\}\,.

We clearly have
|W|\leqslant|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|\leqslant|\Cov_{n,m}|
and
\Cov_{n,m}=W\cup X\cup Y
.
Lemma

C.2
proves
|W\cup X|\gg|Y|
, and since a co-bipartite graph admits a unique covering by two cliques if and only if it has no universal vertex,
Lemma

C.3
proves
|W|\gg|X|
. It follows that
{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)\sim|W|
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

C.1
.

We first calculate the asymptotics of ratios of binomial coefficients. For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
define the set

J_{n}:=\begin{cases}\{2j:j\in\{0,1,\dots\},\,2j\leqslant\sqrt{\log n}\}&n\text{ is even}\\
\{2j+1:j\in\{0,1,\dots\},\,2j+1\leqslant\sqrt{\log n}\}&n\text{ is odd}\end{cases}\,,

so that
J_{n}
is the set of possible imbalances of an almost-equitable bipartition of
n
vertices. For all
k\in J_{n}
we compute that

(60)

\binom{n}{(n+k)/2}\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}^{-1}=\binom{n}{(n-k)/2}\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}^{-1}\sim 1\,,

where the asymptotic relation follows from
Lemma

A.2
and
k=O(\log n)
. We now continue the calculation from
Equation

58
, this time assuming
k\in J_{n}
and using
Lemma

A.2
to obtain asymptotics:

\displaystyle\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}{\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor\cdot\lceil n/2\rceil}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor-\lfloor k^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor-\lfloor k^{2}/4\rfloor+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}^{-1}

(61)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\sim\left(\frac{m-\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor+\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}\right)^{\lfloor k^{2}/4\rfloor}\sim\left(2\gamma-1\right)^{(k^{2}-r)/4}\,

where
r=n\bmod{2}
.
Lemmas

C.2
and
C.3.1
, together with (
60
) and (
61
), imply that if
n
is even, we have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{n/2}\binom{n^{2}/4}{m-2\binom{n/2}{2}}+\sum_{k\in J_{n}\setminus\{0\}}\binom{n}{(n+k)/2}\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}

\displaystyle\sim\binom{n}{n/2}\binom{n^{2}/4}{m-2\binom{n/2}{2}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{k\in J_{n}\setminus\{0\}}\left(2\gamma-1\right)^{k^{2}/4}\Bigg)

(62)

\displaystyle\sim\binom{n}{n/2}\binom{n^{2}/4}{m-2\binom{n/2}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\left(2\gamma-1\right)^{\ell^{2}}\right)\,,

where we substituted
\ell=k/2
. Similarly, if
n
is odd then

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{B}}_{c}(n,m)|

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{k\in J_{n}}\binom{n}{(n+k)/2}\binom{(n-k)(n+k)/4}{m-\binom{(n-k)/2}{2}-\binom{(n+k)/2}{2}}

\displaystyle\sim\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor\cdot\lceil n/2\rceil}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}\sum_{k\in J_{n}}\left(2\gamma-1\right)^{(k^{2}-1)/4}

(63)

\displaystyle\sim\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom{\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor}{m-\binom{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{2}-\binom{\lceil n/2\rceil}{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\left(2\gamma-1\right)^{\ell^{2}+\ell}\,,

where we substituted
\ell=(k-1)/2
. Combining
(
62
)
and
(
63
)
completes the proof.
∎
